\displaystyle\frac{\mathbf{p}}{p_{0}}\mathscr{J}_{(3)}^{0}+\frac{s}{2p_{0}}\bm{\nabla}_{(0)}\times\boldsymbol{\mathscr{J}}_{(2)}-\frac{1}{p_{0}}\Pi_{(2)}^{0}\boldsymbol{\mathscr{J}}_{(1)}

(5.9)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{p_{0}}\bm{\Pi}_{(2)}\mathscr{J}_{(1)}^{0}+\frac{s}{2p_{0}}\bm{\nabla}_{(2)}\times\boldsymbol{\mathscr{J}}_{(0)}\,.

In the first line of Eq. (
5.9
) we see that
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{J}}_{(0)}

is determined by
\mathscr{J}_{(0)}^{0}
. As shown in the second
of Eq. (
5.9
),
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{J}}_{(1)}
depends on
\mathscr{J}_{0}^{(0)}

through
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{J}}^{(0)}
and
\mathscr{J}_{(1)}^{0}
. Similarly

\boldsymbol{\mathscr{J}}^{(2)}
is determined by
(\mathscr{J}_{0}^{(0)},\mathscr{J}_{0}^{(1)},\mathscr{J}_{0}^{(2)})
,
and
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{J}}^{(3)}
is determined by
(\mathscr{J}_{0}^{(0)},\mathscr{J}_{0}^{(1)},\mathscr{J}_{0}^{(2)},\mathscr{J}_{0}^{(3)})
.
So
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{J}}^{(n)}
is determined by
\mathscr{J}_{0}

up to the order
n
.

Let us look at the evolution equation. At the zeroth order, one evolution
equation is the first line of Eq. (
5.5
). Another
evolution equation is from the second equation of Eq. (
)
by inserting the second equation of Eq. (
5.9
),

\nabla_{(0)}^{0}\boldsymbol{\mathscr{J}}_{(0)}+\bm{\nabla}_{(0)}\mathscr{J}_{(0)}^{0}=\frac{1}{p_{0}}\mathbf{p}\times\left(\bm{\nabla}_{(0)}\times\boldsymbol{\mathscr{J}}_{(0)}\right).

(5.10)

One can show that Eq. (
5.10
) is consistent with
the first line of Eq. (
5.5
) and therefore redundant.
At the first order, one evolution equation is the second line of Eq. (
5.5
).
Another evolution equation can be obtained by inserting the third
equation of Eq. (
5.9
) into the third equation of Eq. (
).
One can prove that these two evolution equations are consistent with
each other, so at least one equation is redundant. The same result
can be proved to any order of
\hbar

[
365
]
.

We can solve
\mathscr{J}_{(i)}^{0}
order by order through mass-shell
conditions, which can be done by substituting Eq. (
5.9
)
into Eq. (
5.7
). At the zeroth order, we obtain
from the first equation of Eq. (
5.7
) by using
the first equation of Eq. (
5.9
),

\mathscr{J}_{(0)}^{0}=p_{0}f_{(0)}\delta(p^{2}),

(5.11)

where
f_{(0)}
is an arbitrary scalar function of
x
and
p

without singularity at
p^{2}=0
and can be determined by the first
evolution equation of Eq. (
5.5
). At the first order,
we insert the second equation of Eq. (
5.9
) into the second
equation of Eq. (
5.7
) and obtain

\mathscr{J}_{(1)}^{0}=p_{0}f_{(1)}\delta(p^{2})+s(\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{B})f_{(0)}\delta^{\prime}(p^{2}),

(5.12)

where
f_{(1)}
is an arbitrary scalar function of
x
and
p

without singularity at
p^{2}=0
and can be determined by the second
evolution equation of Eq. (
5.5
). Here we have used
the derivative of a delta function
\delta^{\prime}(y)\equiv d\delta(y)/dy=-\delta(y)/y
.
Similarly one can derive
\mathscr{J}_{(2)}^{0}
as

\displaystyle\mathscr{J}_{(2)}^{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{0}f_{(2)}\delta(p^{2})+s(\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{B})f_{(1)}\delta^{\prime}(p^{2})+\frac{(\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{B})^{2}}{2p_{0}}f_{(0)}\delta^{\prime\prime}(p^{2})

(5.13)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4p^{2}}\mathbf{p}\cdot\left\{\bm{\nabla}_{(0)}\times\left[\frac{1}{p_{0}}\bm{\nabla}_{(0)}\times\left(\mathbf{p}f_{(0)}\delta(p^{2})\right)\right]\right\}-\frac{p_{0}}{p^{2}}\Pi_{\mu}^{(2)}p^{\mu}f_{(0)}\delta(p^{2})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{p^{2}}\mathbf{p}\cdot\left(\bm{\Pi}_{(2)}p_{0}-\Pi_{(2)}^{0}\mathbf{p}\right)f_{(0)}\delta(p^{2})\,,

where
f_{(2)}
is an arbitrary scalar function of
x
and
p

without singularity at
p^{2}=0
and can be determined by the third
evolution equation of Eq. (
5.5
).

We can combine
\mathscr{J}_{(0)}^{0}
,
\mathscr{J}_{(1)}^{0}

and the first three terms of
\mathscr{J}_{(2)}^{0}
to obtain
\mathscr{J}_{0}

up to the second order,

\mathscr{J}^{0}\approx p_{0}f(x,p)\delta\left(p^{2}+s\hbar\frac{\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{B}}{p_{0}}\right),

(5.14)

where
f(x,p)\equiv f_{(0)}(x,p)+\hbar f_{(1)}(x,p)+\hbar^{2}f_{(2)}(x,p)

is the off-shell distribution function up to
O(\hbar^{2})
. We see
that to the first order the energy poles have been shifted to

E_{p}^{(\pm)}=\pm E_{p}(1\mp s\hbar\mathbf{B}\cdot\bm{\Omega}_{p}),

(5.15)

where
E_{p}\equiv E_{\mathbf{p}}=|\mathbf{p}|
is the energy of the free fermion and

\bm{\Omega}_{p}\equiv\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{p}}=\mathbf{p}/(2|\mathbf{p}|^{3})
is
the Berry curvature in momentum space. The energy correction can be
regarded as the magnetic moment energy of chiral fermions
[
145
,
155
,
148
,
363
]
.

5.2  Chiral kinetic equation for on-shell fermions

In the former subsection we
have shown that all components of the chiral Wigner function can be
expressed by its time component in the background electromagnetic
field order by order in
\hbar
. In this subsection, we will derive
the CKE in terms of the single-component distribution function from
the evolution equations in Eq. (
5.5
).

We start from the zeroth order equation, the first equation of Eq. (
5.5
),

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}^{(0)}\mathscr{J}_{(0)}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{\mu}^{x}-F_{\mu\nu}\partial_{p}^{\nu}\right)\mathscr{J}_{(0)}^{\mu}

(5.16)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{\mu}^{x}-F_{\mu\nu}\partial_{p}^{\nu}\right)[p^{\mu}f_{(0)}\delta(p^{2})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{t}+\mathbf{E}\cdot\nabla_{p}\right)[p_{0}f_{(0)}\delta(p^{2})]

\displaystyle+\left(\nabla_{x}+\mathbf{E}\partial_{p_{0}}+\mathbf{B}\times\nabla_{p}\right)\cdot[\mathbf{p}f_{(0)}\delta(p^{2})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

where we have used
\mathscr{J}_{(0)}^{\mu}=p^{\mu}f_{(0)}\delta(p^{2})

from Eqs. (
5.9
) and (
5.11
). The chiral kinetic
equation for on-shell fermions or anti-fermions can be obtained by
an integration of Eq. (
5.16
) over
p_{0}

in the range
[0,\infty)
or
(-\infty,0]
respectively. Then for
on-shell fermions, the chiral kinetic equation reads

\left(\partial_{t}+\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla_{x}\right)f_{(0)}+(\mathbf{E}+\mathbf{v}\times\mathbf{B})\cdot\nabla_{p}f_{(0)}=0,

(5.17)

where
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{p}/E_{p}
is the velocity of the fermion.
In deriving the above equation, the term with
\partial_{p_{0}}

in Eq. (
5.16
) gives a vanishing result in
the integration over
p_{0}
from the boundary condition.

Before the integration over
p_{0}
, the first order equation is
provided by the second equation of Eq. (
5.5
). We
can add the zeroth and first order equations, i.e. the first two equations
of Eq. (
5.5
), and obtain the evolution equation
up to
O(\hbar)
,

p_{\mu}\nabla_{(0)}^{\mu}\left[f\delta(\widetilde{p}^{2})\right]+\hbar\frac{s}{2}\bm{\nabla}_{(0)}\cdot\left\{\frac{1}{p_{0}}\bm{\nabla}_{(0)}\times\left[\mathbf{p}f_{(0)}\delta(p^{2})\right]\right\}=0,

(5.18)

where
\widetilde{p}^{2}\equiv p^{2}+s\hbar(\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{B})/p_{0}

and
f\equiv f_{(0)}(x,p)+\hbar f_{(1)}(x,p)
is the off-shell distribution
function up to
O(\hbar)
. After the integration of the above equation
over
p_{0}
in the range
[0,\infty)
, we obtain the CKE for on-shell
fermions (not antifermions) with non-vanishing momenta up to
O(\hbar)

[
145
,
155
,
156
,
151
,
152
]
,

\displaystyle\left(1+\hbar s\bm{\Omega}_{p}\cdot\mathbf{B}\right)\partial_{t}f(x,E_{p},\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle+\left[\mathbf{v}+\hbar s(\mathbf{E}\times\bm{\Omega}_{p})+\hbar s\frac{1}{2|\mathbf{p}|^{2}}\mathbf{B}\right]\cdot\bm{\nabla}_{x}f(x,E_{p},\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle+\left[\widetilde{\mathbf{E}}+\mathbf{v}\times\mathbf{B}+\hbar s(\mathbf{E}\cdot\mathbf{B})\bm{\Omega}_{p}\right]\cdot\bm{\nabla}_{p}f(x,E_{p},\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(5.19)

where
\widetilde{\mathbf{E}}\equiv\mathbf{E}-\bm{\nabla}_{x}E_{p}^{(+)}

is the effective electric field,
\mathbf{v}\equiv\bm{\nabla}_{p}E_{p}^{(+)}

is the effective velocity, and
f(x,E_{p},\mathbf{p})
is the on-shell
distribution function up to
O(\hbar)
.

Note that in the integration over
p_{0}
in the range
[0,\infty)

there are some infrared singular terms from the derivative in
p_{0}

that cannot be dropped causally. It turns out that two additional
terms in the above CKE appear, which are singular at
|\mathbf{p}|=0

but were previously neglected
[
365
]
. These new terms
do not contribute to the CKE at non-vanishing momentum, but it is
present in the anomalous conservation equation obtained by an integration
over
p_{0}
in the full range
(-\infty,\infty)

[
365
]
.
This may imply that the chiral anomaly may arise from another source
other than the well-known Berry phase in three-momentum, which seems
to be consistent with the observation of Ref.
[
154
]
.

5.3  Wigner functions in a general Lorentz frame

In Secs
5.1
and

5.2
, we work in a specific Lorentz
frame. It is straightforward to introduce a time-like frame vector

n^{\mu}
to rewrite all formulas in a general Lorentz frame, where

n^{\mu}
behaves like a four-velocity satisfying
n^{2}=1
. For
simplicity, we assume
n^{\mu}
is a constant vector. Then we can
decompose any vector
X^{\mu}
into the component parallel to
n^{\mu}

and that perpendicular to
n^{\mu}
,
X^{\mu}=(X\cdot n)n^{\mu}+\overline{X}^{\mu}

with
\overline{X}\cdot n=0
. For
n^{\mu}=(1,0,0,0)
we have the
normal decomposition:
X\cdot n=X_{0}
and
\overline{X}^{\mu}=(0,\mathbf{X})
.
In such a decomposition, we can rewrite Eqs. (
5.5
)-(
5.8
)
in a general Lorentz frame with the frame vector
n^{\mu}
. Corresponding
to Eqs. (
5.9
), (
5.11
), and (
5.12
),
the zeroth and first-order solutions to the chiral Wigner function
in a general Lorentz frame read

\displaystyle\mathscr{J}_{(0)}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p^{\mu}f_{(0)}\delta(p^{2}),

\displaystyle\mathscr{J}_{(1)}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p^{\mu}\frac{n\cdot\mathscr{J}_{(1)}}{n\cdot p}-\frac{s}{2(n\cdot p)}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}n_{\nu}\nabla_{\sigma}\mathscr{J}_{(0)\rho}

(5.20)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p^{\mu}f_{(1)}\delta(p^{2})-sp^{\mu}\frac{1}{n\cdot p}\widetilde{F}_{\lambda\nu}n^{\nu}p^{\lambda}f_{(0)}\delta^{\prime}(p^{2})

\displaystyle-\frac{s}{2(n\cdot p)}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}n_{\nu}\nabla_{\sigma}\mathscr{J}_{(0)\rho},

where we have used

\frac{n\cdot\mathscr{J}_{(1)}}{n\cdot p}=f_{(1)}\delta(p^{2})-s\frac{1}{n\cdot p}\widetilde{F}_{\lambda\nu}n^{\nu}p^{\lambda}f_{(0)}\delta^{\prime}(p^{2}).

(5.21)

Then the chiral Wigner function up to the first order in
\hbar

has the form,

\displaystyle\mathscr{J}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{J}_{(0)}^{\mu}+\hbar\mathscr{J}_{(1)}^{\mu}

(5.22)

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle p^{\mu}f\delta\left(p^{2}-\hbar s\frac{1}{n\cdot p}\widetilde{F}_{\lambda\nu}n^{\nu}p^{\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle-\hbar s\frac{1}{n\cdot p}\widetilde{F}^{\mu\lambda}n_{\lambda}f_{(0)}\delta(p^{2})

\displaystyle-\hbar s\frac{1}{2(n\cdot p)}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}n_{\nu}p_{\rho}\nabla_{\sigma}[f_{(0)}\delta(p^{2})],

where
f\equiv f_{(0)}+\hbar f_{(1)}
.

One has a freedom to choose another frame with a different velocity

n_{\mu}^{\prime}
, then
\mathscr{J}_{(0)}^{\mu}
and
\mathscr{J}_{(1)}^{\mu}

in Eq. (
5.20
) have the form,

\displaystyle\mathscr{J}_{(0)}^{\prime\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p^{\mu}f_{(0)}\delta(p^{2}),

\displaystyle\mathscr{J}_{(1)}^{\prime\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p^{\mu}f_{(1)}^{\prime}\delta(p^{2})-sp^{\mu}\frac{1}{n^{\prime}\cdot p}\widetilde{F}_{\lambda\nu}n^{\prime\nu}p^{\lambda}f_{(0)}\delta^{\prime}(p^{2})

(5.23)

\displaystyle-\frac{s}{2(n^{\prime}\cdot p)}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}n_{\nu}^{\prime}\nabla_{\sigma}\mathscr{J}_{(0)\rho},

where
f_{(1)}^{\prime}
is the first-order distribution function
corresponding to the frame
n_{\mu}^{\prime}
(the prime does not
denote the derivative). One can verify both
\mathscr{J}_{(0)}^{\mu}

and
\mathscr{J}_{(1)}^{\mu}
are independent of the choice of the
frame vector
n^{\mu}
,

\displaystyle\mathscr{J}_{(0)}^{\prime\mu}-\mathscr{J}_{(0)}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\mathscr{J}_{(1)}^{\prime\mu}-\mathscr{J}_{(1)}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

(5.24)

The first equation of Eq. (
5.24
) is obvious since

\mathscr{J}_{(0)}^{\mu}
does not have an explicit dependence on

n^{\mu}
, which also means that
f_{(0)}
does not have the frame
dependence. It takes more effort to prove the second equation of Eq. (
5.24
)

[
365
]
which implies that
f_{(1)}
is not invariant
with respect to the frame change. The change of
n\cdot\mathscr{J}_{(1)}/(n\cdot p)

has the form,

\displaystyle\delta\left(\frac{n\cdot\mathscr{J}_{(1)}}{n\cdot p}\right)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\frac{n^{\prime}\cdot\mathscr{J}_{(1)}}{n^{\prime}\cdot p}-\frac{n\cdot\mathscr{J}_{(1)}}{n\cdot p}

(5.25)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n_{\lambda}n_{\nu}^{\prime}\left(p^{\lambda}\mathscr{J}_{(1)}^{\nu}-p^{\nu}\mathscr{J}_{(1)}^{\lambda}\right)}{(n\cdot p)(n^{\prime}\cdot p)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-s\frac{\epsilon^{\lambda\nu\rho\sigma}n_{\lambda}n_{\nu}^{\prime}\nabla_{\rho}\mathscr{J}_{(0)\sigma}}{2(n\cdot p)(n^{\prime}\cdot p)},

where we have used Eq. (
4.8
) at the first order. On
the other hand the change of
n\cdot\mathscr{J}_{(1)}/(n\cdot p)

has two contributions according to Eq. (
5.21
),

\delta\left(\frac{n\cdot\mathscr{J}_{(1)}}{n\cdot p}\right)=\delta f_{(1)}\delta(p^{2})-s\left(\frac{\widetilde{F}_{\lambda\nu}n^{\prime\nu}}{n^{\prime}\cdot p}-\frac{\widetilde{F}_{\lambda\nu}n^{\nu}}{n\cdot p}\right)p^{\lambda}f_{(0)}\delta^{\prime}(p^{2}),

(5.26)

where we have defined
\delta f_{(1)}\equiv f_{(1)}^{\prime}-f_{(1)}
.
From Eqs. (
5.25
) and (
5.26
),
we obtain the variation of the distribution function due to the change
of the frame as

\displaystyle\delta f_{(1)}\delta(p^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s\left(\frac{\widetilde{F}_{\lambda\nu}n^{\prime\nu}}{n^{\prime}\cdot p}-\frac{\widetilde{F}_{\lambda\nu}n^{\nu}}{n\cdot p}\right)p^{\lambda}f_{(0)}\delta^{\prime}(p^{2})

(5.27)

\displaystyle-s\frac{\epsilon^{\lambda\nu\rho\sigma}n_{\lambda}n_{\nu}^{\prime}\nabla_{\rho}[p_{\sigma}f_{(0)}\delta(p^{2})]}{2(n\cdot p)(n^{\prime}\cdot p)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-s\frac{\epsilon^{\lambda\nu\rho\sigma}n_{\lambda}n_{\nu}^{\prime}p_{\sigma}}{2(n\cdot p)(n^{\prime}\cdot p)}\delta(p^{2})\nabla_{\rho}f_{(0)},

where only the term proportional to
\nabla_{\rho}f_{(0)}
survives
while other terms cancel in the second equality. Equation (
5.27
)
is related to the side-jump effect
[
149
,
151
]
.

5.4  Decomposition of CME and CVE current

In Sec.
4.2

we derive the chiral Wigner function in the covariant form up to the second
order in
\hbar
under the equilibrium conditions (
4.18
)-(
4.20
).
The results are given in Eqs. (
4.15
), (
4.28
),
and (
4.29
). When carrying out an integration
over the four-momentum for the chiral Wigner function, we obtain the
chiral current in Eq. (
4.10
) and then the charge
(vector) and axial charge (vector) currents in Eq. (
4.31
).
The results for the charge and axial charge currents in Eqs. (
4.32
)
and (
4.33
) show that they all have explicit
Lorentz covariance.

The first-order charge current
J_{(1)}^{\mu}=\xi\omega^{\mu}+\xi_{B}B^{\mu}

in Eq. (
4.32
) gives the CME and CVE. In
the semiclassical expansion without assuming equilibrium conditions
and constant electromagnetic fields, one can still solve the vector
component
\mathscr{J}^{\mu}
of the Wigner function order by order
in
\hbar
as we have shown in Sec.
5
. In
this formalism, to any order of
\hbar
, only the time component
of
\mathscr{J}^{\mu}
is independent, while spatial components can
be derived from the time component. There is a freedom to choose any
reference frame in which the time component is defined. So the explicit
Lorentz covariance seems to be lost.

In this section, we will show that under equilibrium conditions (
4.18
)-(
4.20
),
the CVE current can be decomposed into two parts, which can be identified
as the normal current and magnetization current. Each part depends
on the reference frame, but the sum of two parts does give the total
CVE current in a Lorentz covariant form without the frame dependence

[
364
]
. Similar properties also exist for the CME current.

We start from the application of the equilibrium conditions (
4.18
)-(
4.20
)
to
\delta f_{(1)}\delta(p^{2})
in Eq. (
5.27
)

\displaystyle\delta f_{(1)}\delta(p^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-s\frac{\epsilon^{\lambda\nu\rho\sigma}n_{\lambda}n_{\nu}^{\prime}p_{\sigma}}{2(n\cdot p)(n^{\prime}\cdot p)}\delta(p^{2})\nabla_{\rho}f_{(0)}

(5.28)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-s\frac{p^{\mu}\epsilon^{\lambda\nu\rho\sigma}n_{\lambda}n_{\nu}^{\prime}p_{\sigma}\Omega_{\rho\mu}}{2\left(n^{\prime}\cdot p\right)\left(n\cdot p\right)}\frac{df_{(0)}}{d(\beta\cdot p)}\delta(p^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-s\frac{n_{\alpha}^{\prime}p_{\gamma}\widetilde{\Omega}^{\alpha\gamma}}{2\left(n^{\prime}\cdot p\right)}\frac{df_{(0)}}{d(\beta\cdot p)}\delta(p^{2})+s\frac{n_{\alpha}p_{\gamma}\widetilde{\Omega}^{\alpha\gamma}}{2\left(n\cdot p\right)}\frac{df_{(0)}}{d(\beta\cdot p)}\delta(p^{2}),

where we have used following identity in equilibrium,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\rho}f_{(0)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{df_{(0)}}{d(\beta\cdot p)}\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{\rho}\beta_{\mu}-\partial_{\mu}\beta_{\rho})p^{\mu}

(5.29)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{df_{(0)}}{d(\beta\cdot p)}\Omega_{\rho\mu}p^{\mu}.

The derivation of the above identity can be found in intermediate steps
of Eq. (
4.17
). Obviously
\delta f_{(1)}
in
Eq. (
5.28
) depends on the frame, but we single
out the contribution with an explicit frame dependence from that independent
of the frame as

\displaystyle f_{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{(1)}-s\frac{n_{\alpha}p_{\gamma}\widetilde{\Omega}^{\alpha\gamma}}{2\left(n\cdot p\right)}\frac{df_{(0)}}{d(\beta\cdot p)},

(5.30)

where
\widetilde{f}_{(1)}
does not depend on the reference vector

n^{\mu}
, i.e.
\delta\widetilde{f}_{(1)}=0
. A simple choice of
a specific solution is
\widetilde{f}_{(1)}=0
.

Substituting Eq. (
5.30
) into Eq. (
5.20
) yields

\displaystyle\mathscr{J}_{(1)}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{J}_{(1)B}^{\mu}+\mathscr{J}_{(1)\omega}^{\mu},

(5.31)

where

\displaystyle\mathscr{J}_{(1)B}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{s}{n\cdot p}p^{\mu}n_{\nu}\widetilde{F}^{\nu\lambda}p_{\lambda}f_{(0)}\delta^{\prime}\left(p^{2}\right)

(5.32)

\displaystyle+\frac{s}{2n\cdot p}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}n_{\nu}F_{\sigma\lambda}f_{(0)}\partial_{p}^{\lambda}\left[p_{\rho}\delta\left(p^{2}\right)\right],

\displaystyle\mathscr{J}_{(1)\omega}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-p^{\mu}\frac{s}{2\left(n\cdot p\right)}n_{\alpha}p_{\gamma}\widetilde{\Omega}^{\alpha\gamma}\frac{df_{(0)}}{d(\beta\cdot p)}\delta(p^{2})

(5.33)

\displaystyle-\frac{s}{2n\cdot p}p^{\lambda}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}n_{\nu}p_{\rho}\Omega_{\sigma\lambda}\frac{df_{(0)}}{d(\beta\cdot p)}\delta(p^{2}).

We see that
\mathscr{J}_{(1)B}^{\mu}
and
\mathscr{J}_{(1)\omega}^{\mu}

have superficial dependence on the frame vector
n^{\mu}
, and the
first term of
\mathscr{J}_{(1)B}^{\mu}
in Eq. (
5.32
)
is proportional to
p^{\mu}
while the second one is perpendicular
to it, so is
\mathscr{J}_{(1)\omega}^{\mu}
in Eq. (
5.33
).
However one can prove that
\mathscr{J}_{(1)B}^{\mu}
in Eq. (
5.32
)
and
\mathscr{J}_{(1)\omega}^{\mu}
in Eq. (
5.33
)
do give the covariant result in Eq. (
4.28
),
which are frame independent
[
364
]
.

In order to show the physical meaning of
\mathscr{J}_{(1)B}^{\mu}

and
\mathscr{J}_{(1)\omega}^{\mu}
in Eqs. (
5.32
)
and (
5.33
), let us evaluate the current
by assuming that
n^{\mu}
is the fluid velocity
u^{\mu}
. For
the CME current, it is easy to rewrite the currents corresponding
to two terms of
\mathscr{J}_{(1)B}^{\mu}
in Eq. (
5.32
)
which we denote as
J_{s,B}^{\mu}(1\mathrm{st})
and
J_{s,B}^{\mu}(2\mathrm{nd})
,

\displaystyle J_{s,B}^{\mu}(1\mathrm{st})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hbar su_{\nu}\widetilde{F}^{\nu\lambda}\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{p_{0}}p^{\mu}p_{\lambda}f_{(0)}\delta^{\prime}\left(p^{2}\right)

(5.34)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\hbar sB^{\lambda}\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{p_{0}^{3}}\overline{p}^{\mu}\overline{p}_{\lambda}f_{(0)}\delta\left(p^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+\hbar\frac{1}{2}sB^{\lambda}\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{p_{0}^{2}}\overline{p}^{\mu}\overline{p}_{\lambda}\frac{df_{(0)}}{dp_{0}}\delta\left(p^{2}\right),

\displaystyle J_{s,B}^{\mu}(2\mathrm{nd})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hbar\frac{s}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}u_{\nu}F_{\sigma\lambda}\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{p_{0}}f_{(0)}\partial_{p}^{\lambda}\left[p_{\rho}\delta\left(p^{2}\right)\right]

(5.35)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\hbar sB^{\mu}\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{p_{0}}f_{(0)}\delta\left(p^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+\hbar s\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}u_{\nu}F_{\sigma\lambda}\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{p_{0}^{3}}\overline{p}_{\rho}\overline{p}^{\lambda}f_{(0)}\delta\left(p^{2}\right)

\displaystyle-\hbar\frac{s}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}u_{\nu}F_{\sigma\lambda}\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{p_{0}^{2}}\overline{p}_{\rho}\overline{p}^{\lambda}\frac{df_{(0)}}{dp_{0}}\delta\left(p^{2}\right),

where
p_{0}\equiv u\cdot p
,
\overline{p}^{\mu}\equiv p^{\mu}-p_{0}u^{\mu}
,

\xi_{B,s}=(\xi_{B}+s\xi_{B5})/2
is the CME coefficient corresponding
to the chirality
s
, and
f_{(0)}
is given by Eq. (
4.16
)
but without the unity in the anti-particle sector proportional to

\Theta(-p_{0})
. Note that we have put back
\hbar
in
J_{s,B}^{\mu}(1\mathrm{st})

and
J_{s,B}^{\mu}(2\mathrm{nd})
. It can be proved that
J_{s,B}^{\mu}(2\mathrm{nd})=0
,
i.e. the current from the second term gives vanishing result. Now
we analyze the two terms of
J_{s,B}^{\mu}(1\mathrm{st})
in Eq. (
5.34
)
which we denote as
J_{s,B}^{\mu}(a)
and
J_{s,B}^{\mu}(b)
,

\displaystyle J_{s,B}^{\mu}(a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\hbar sB^{\lambda}\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{p_{0}^{2}}\overline{p}^{\mu}\overline{p}_{\lambda}\frac{df_{(0)}}{dp_{0}}\delta\left(p^{2}\right)

(5.36)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\hbar s\beta\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{E_{p}^{3}}\overline{p}^{\mu}(\overline{p}\cdot B)

\displaystyle\times\left[f_{\mathrm{FD}}^{+}(1-f_{\mathrm{FD}}^{+})-f_{\mathrm{FD}}^{-}(1-f_{\mathrm{FD}}^{-})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\xi_{Bs}B^{\mu},

\displaystyle J_{s,B}^{\mu}(b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\hbar sB^{\lambda}\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{p_{0}^{3}}\overline{p}^{\mu}\overline{p}_{\lambda}f_{(0)}\delta\left(p^{2}\right)

(5.37)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\hbar s\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{E_{p}^{4}}\overline{p}^{\mu}(\overline{p}\cdot B)\left(f_{\mathrm{FD}}^{+}-f_{\mathrm{FD}}^{-}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}\xi_{Bs}B^{\mu},

where
E_{p}=|\mathbf{p}|
and
f_{\mathrm{FD}}^{\pm}\equiv f_{\mathrm{FD}}(E_{p}\mp\mu_{s})
.
We see that
J_{s,B}^{\mu}(a)
and
J_{s,B}^{\mu}(b)
give one-third
and two-thirds of the CME, respectively. In order to go further to
show the physical meaning of
J_{s,B}^{\mu}(a)
and
J_{s,B}^{\mu}(b)
,
we can rewrite them in the local rest frame with
u^{\mu}=(1,0,0,0)
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}_{s,B}(a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hbar s\beta\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{2|\mathbf{p}|^{3}}\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{B})

\displaystyle\times\left[f_{\mathrm{FD}}^{+}(1-f_{\mathrm{FD}}^{+})-f_{\mathrm{FD}}^{-}(1-f_{\mathrm{FD}}^{-})\right]

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{\mathbf{p}}{|\mathbf{p}|}\left[f_{\mathrm{FD}}\left(|\mathbf{p}|-\mu_{s}-s\hbar\frac{\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{B}}{2|\mathbf{p}|^{2}}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+f_{\mathrm{FD}}\left(|\mathbf{p}|+\mu_{s}+s\hbar\frac{\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{B}}{2|\mathbf{p}|^{2}}\right)\right],

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}_{s,B}(b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hbar s\beta\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{p}|^{4}}\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{B})

(5.38)

\displaystyle\times\left[f_{\mathrm{FD}}(E_{p}-\mu_{s})-f_{\mathrm{FD}}(E_{p}+\mu_{s})\right].

We see that the current
\mathbf{J}_{s,B}(a)
comes from the three-momentum
integral of the velocity
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{p}/|\mathbf{p}|

on the Fermi-Dirac distribution, which includes the energy correction
from the magnetic moment in the magnetic field with the magnetic moment
being
\hbar sQ\mathbf{p}/(2|\mathbf{p}|^{2})
for fermions/anti-fermions
with the electric charge
Q=\pm
respectively. According to Ref.

[
371
]
,
\mathbf{J}_{s,B}(b)
is from the modification
of the quasi-particle velocity due to the Berry curvature.

In a similar way, we can analyze the CVE current from
\mathscr{J}_{(1)\omega}^{\mu}

in Eq. (
5.33
), in which the two terms
are denoted as
J_{s,\omega}^{\mu}(a)
and
J_{s,\omega}^{\mu}(b)
.
Their results are

\displaystyle J_{s,\omega}^{\mu}(a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\hbar su_{\alpha}\widetilde{\Omega}^{\alpha\gamma}\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{p_{0}}p^{\mu}p_{\gamma}\frac{df_{s}}{d(\beta\cdot p)}\delta(p^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\hbar s\beta\omega^{\gamma}\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{\overline{p}^{\mu}\overline{p}_{\gamma}}{E_{p}^{2}}\left[f_{\mathrm{FD}}^{+}(1-f_{\mathrm{FD}}^{+})+f_{\mathrm{FD}}^{-}(1-f_{\mathrm{FD}}^{-})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\xi_{s}\omega^{\mu},

\displaystyle J_{s,\omega}^{\mu}(b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\hbar s\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}u_{\nu}\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{p_{0}}p_{\rho}(\partial_{\sigma}^{x}f_{s})\delta(p^{2})

(5.39)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}\xi_{s}\omega^{\mu}.

We see that
J_{s,\omega}^{\mu}(a)
and
J_{s,\omega}^{\mu}(b)

contribute to the full CVE current by 1/3 and 2/3, respectively. By
choosing the local rest frame
u^{\mu}=(1,0,0,0)
at one space-time
point but with
\partial_{\mu}u_{\nu}\neq 0
in its vicinity, we obtain
the three-dimension form for the two terms,

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}_{s,\omega}(a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\hbar s\beta\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{\mathbf{p}}{|\mathbf{p}|^{2}}(\mathbf{p}\cdot\bm{\omega})\left[f_{\mathrm{FD}}^{+}(1-f_{\mathrm{FD}}^{+})+f_{\mathrm{FD}}^{-}(1-f_{\mathrm{FD}}^{-})\right]

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{\mathbf{p}}{|\mathbf{p}|}\left[f_{\mathrm{FD}}\left(|\mathbf{p}|-\mu_{s}-s\hbar\frac{\mathbf{p}\cdot\bm{\omega}}{2|\mathbf{p}|}\right)\right.\left.+f_{\mathrm{FD}}\left(|\mathbf{p}|+\mu_{s}-s\hbar\frac{\mathbf{p}\cdot\bm{\omega}}{2|\mathbf{p}|}\right)\right],

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}_{s,\omega}(b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{|\mathbf{v}|=0}\nabla\times\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\left(\frac{s\mathbf{p}}{2|\mathbf{p}|^{2}}\hbar\right)

(5.40)

\displaystyle\times\left[f_{\mathrm{FD}}(\gamma|\mathbf{p}|-\gamma\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{p}-\mu_{s})+f_{\mathrm{FD}}(\gamma|\mathbf{p}|-\gamma\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{p}+\mu_{s})\right],

where in obtaining
\mathbf{J}_{s,\omega}(b)
we have taken the limit
at
|\mathbf{v}|=0
with
u^{\mu}=(\gamma,\gamma\mathbf{v})
and

\gamma=1/\sqrt{1-|\mathbf{v}|^{2}}
. We can see that
\mathbf{J}_{s,\omega}(a)

comes from the momentum integration of the fermion’s velocity
\mathbf{v}_{p}

with the Fermi-Dirac distribution function in which the fermion’s
energy is modified by the spin-vorticity coupling, while
\mathbf{J}_{s,\omega}(b)

is the “magnetization” current due to the “magnetic” moment
of chiral fermions in vorticity fields
[
148
]
. Note
that the energy correction from the spin-vorticity coupling has the
same sign for fermions and anti-fermions.

6  Chiral kinetic theory from effective theories

In this section, we briefly introduce other effective theories related to the chiral transport phenomena. We first introduce the concept of Berry phase and Berry curvature in quantum mechanics. We then derive the kinetic theory with quantum corrections proportional to Berry curvature, which is the 3-dimensional chiral kinetic theory, by the path integrals of the effective Hamiltonian of chiral fermions. Later, we discuss the symplectic form for the phase space in the presence of electromagnetic fields coupled to the Berry curvature and compute the modified Poisson bracket to derive the chiral kinetic theory. After that, we consider the Lorentz transformation to the effective action of chiral fermions and obtain the quantum corrections to both space and momentum under Lorentz transformation, i.e., named side jump effects. We also introduce the high-density effective theory and comment on other related methods.
For simple notations, we choose
\hbar=1
in this section.

6.1  Berry phase

In this subsection, we briefly introduce the
idea of the Berry phase
[
162
,
164
]
.

We consider a Hamiltonian
H(t)
whose time dependence is through
a set of parameters
\mathbf{R}
,

H=H(\mathbf{R}(t)).

(6.1)

As we will show later,
\mathbf{R}
can be the momentum operator

\mathbf{p}
. We consider an adiabatic evolution of the system:
\mathbf{R}(t)

moves so slowly along a path
C
that the instantaneous orthonormal
basis can be defined at any time
t
,

H(\mathbf{R}(t))\left|n(\mathbf{R}(t))\right\rangle=\varepsilon_{n}(\mathbf{R}(t))\left|n(\mathbf{R}(t))\right\rangle,

(6.2)

where
\left|n(\mathbf{R}(t))\right\rangle
and
\varepsilon_{n}(\mathbf{R}(t))

denote the eigenstate and eigenvalue of
H(\mathbf{R}(t))
at
t

respectively. Different from normal eigenvectors of a time independent
Hamiltonian, the extra phase factor of
\left|n(\mathbf{R}(t))\right\rangle

can be physical and cannot be simply ignored. The wave function has
the form,

\left|\psi_{n}(t)\right\rangle=e^{i\gamma_{n}(t)}e^{-ih(t)}\left|n(\mathbf{R}(t))\right\rangle,

(6.3)

where
e^{-ih(t)}=\exp\left[-i\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\varepsilon_{n}(\mathbf{R}(t^{\prime}))\right]

is usually called the dynamical phase factor and
\gamma_{n}(t)

is the extra phase factor. Inserting the wave function (
6.3
)
into the Schrödinger equation
i\partial_{t}\psi=H\psi
and multiplying
it from the left by
\left\langle n(\mathbf{R}(t))\right|
we obtain

\gamma_{n}=\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\frac{d\mathbf{R}(t^{\prime})}{dt^{\prime}}\cdot\left\langle n(\mathbf{R}(t^{\prime}))\right|\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{R}}\left|n(\mathbf{R}(t^{\prime}))\right\rangle\equiv\int_{c}d\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{a}_{n}(\mathbf{R}),

(6.4)

where we have defined the Berry connection or potential as

\mathbf{a}_{n}(\mathbf{R})=i\left\langle n(\mathbf{R}(t^{\prime}))\right|\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{R}}\left|n(\mathbf{R}(t^{\prime}))\right\rangle.

(6.5)

Similar to the vector potential
\mathbf{A}
for electromagnetic
fields, the Berry connection
\mathbf{a}_{n}
is not gauge invariant.
Under the transformation
\left|n(\mathbf{R})\right\rangle\rightarrow e^{i\xi(\mathbf{R})}\left|n(\mathbf{R})\right\rangle
,
Berry connection transforms as
\mathbf{a}_{n}\rightarrow\mathbf{a}_{n}-\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\xi(\mathbf{R})

and the phase factor changes as
\gamma_{n}\rightarrow\gamma_{n}+\xi(\mathbf{R}(0))-\xi(\mathbf{R}(t))
.
Therefore, one can always choose a suitable
\xi(\mathbf{R}(0))-\xi(\mathbf{R}(t))

to make
\gamma_{n}
vanish. However, if one chooses a closed path,
i.e.
\xi(\mathbf{R}(0))=\xi(\mathbf{R}(t))
or
\xi(\mathbf{R}(0))=\xi(\mathbf{R}(t))+2\pi l

with
l
being an integer, then the phase factor,

\gamma_{c}=\ointop_{C}d\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{a}_{n}(\mathbf{R}),

(6.6)

cannot be removed. Such a gauge-invariant phase is called the Berry
phase after Michael Berry
[
162
]
.

If the parameter space for
\mathbf{R}
has three dimensions, one
can also rewrite
\gamma_{c}
as

\gamma_{c}=\int_{S}d\mathbf{S}\cdot\bm{\Omega}_{n}(\mathbf{R}),

(6.7)

where
S
is the area surrounded by the

closed loop C and

\bm{\Omega}_{n}(\mathbf{R})=\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\times\mathbf{a}_{n}(\mathbf{R)},

(6.8)

is called the Berry curvature.

6.2  Chiral kinetic theory from path integrals

In this subsection, we construct the chiral kinetic theory for massless
fermions incorporating the Berry phase through path integrals
[
146
,
147
,
148
]
.

We consider a spin-
1/2
massless particle moving in a background
electromagnetic field with the vector potential
A^{\mu}=(\phi(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}))
.
The Hamiltonian for the right-handed fermions can be expressed as

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{\sigma}\cdot\left[\mathbf{p}_{c}-e\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x})\right]+e\phi(\mathbf{x}),

(6.9)

where
\mathbf{p}_{c}=\mathbf{p}+e\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x})
is the
canonical momentum with
\mathbf{p}
being the mechanical one, and

\bm{\sigma}
are Pauli matrices
\bm{\sigma}=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3})
.
Note that the Hamiltonian
H
is a
2\times 2
matrix.

Now, we compute the quantum correction to the equation of motion for
particles via the path integral quantization. The transition matrix
element in the path integral is

\displaystyle K_{\text{fi}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{x}_{\text{f}}|e^{-iH(t_{\text{f}}-t_{\text{i}})}|\mathbf{x}_{\text{i}}\rangle

(6.10)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int[D\mathbf{x}][D\mathbf{p}_{c}]P\exp\left[i\int_{t_{\text{i}}}^{t_{\text{f}}}dt(\mathbf{p}_{c}\cdot\dot{\mathbf{x}}-H)\right],

where
t_{\mathrm{i}}
and
t_{\mathrm{f}}
denote the initial and
final time respectively,
\mathbf{x}(t_{\mathrm{i}})=\mathbf{x}_{\text{i}}

and
\mathbf{x}(t_{\text{f}})=\mathbf{x}_{\text{f}}
denote the initial
and final position of the path respectively,
[D\mathbf{x}][D\mathbf{p}_{c}]

denotes the path integral elements in phase space,
\dot{\mathbf{x}}\equiv d\mathbf{x}/dt
,
and
P
is the path ordering operator.

We note that there are two eigenstates of
H
in Eq. (
6.9
),

\sigma\cdot\mathbf{p}\chi_{\pm}(\mathbf{p})=\pm|\mathbf{p}|\chi_{\pm}(\mathbf{p}),\qquad\chi_{\pm}^{\dagger}\chi_{\pm}=1,

(6.11)

Here, the lower index
\pm
denotes the particle (upper) and anti-particle
(lower) respectively. Our propose is to derive the effective action
for right-handed particle or anti-particle only. Therefore, we need
to diagonalize
H
by using a unitary matrix
U_{\mathbf{p}}
with

\mathbf{p}=\mathbf{p}_{c}-e\mathbf{A}
,

\displaystyle U_{\mathbf{p}}^{\dagger}HU_{\mathbf{p}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}|\mathbf{p}|+e\phi(\mathbf{x})&0\\
0&-|\mathbf{p}|+e\phi(\mathbf{x})\end{array}\right]

(6.15)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma_{3}\epsilon(\mathbf{p}_{c}-e\mathbf{A})+e\phi(\mathbf{x}),

where
\epsilon(\mathbf{p})\equiv|\mathbf{p}|
is the particle’s
energy and

U_{\mathbf{p}}=(\chi_{+},\chi_{-})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{-i\phi}\cos\frac{\theta}{2}&-e^{-i\phi}\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\\
\sin\frac{\theta}{2}&\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\end{array}\right),\quad U_{\mathbf{p}}^{\dagger}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\chi_{+}^{\dagger}\\
\chi_{-}^{\dagger}\end{array}\right),

(6.16)

where
\theta
and
\phi
are the polar and azimuthal angle of
\mathbf{p}

respectively. Note that when using
\mathbf{p}_{c}
to label
U_{\mathbf{p}}

as we will do shortly we denote
U_{\mathbf{p}}
as
U(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p}_{c})

since
\mathbf{p}
contains
\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x})
.

Inserting Eqs. (
6.15
,
6.16
) into Eq. (
6.10
)
yields

\displaystyle K_{\text{fi}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int\left[\prod_{j=1}^{N}d\mathbf{x}_{j}d\mathbf{p}_{j}^{c}\right]d\mathbf{x}_{0}\langle\mathbf{x}_{\text{f}}|\mathbf{x}_{N}\rangle

(6.17)

\displaystyle\times\left(\prod_{j=1}^{N}\langle\mathbf{x}_{j}|e^{-3iH\Delta t}|\mathbf{p}_{j}^{c}\rangle\langle\mathbf{p}_{j}^{c}|e^{-3iH\Delta t}|\mathbf{x}_{j-1}\rangle\right)\langle\mathbf{x}_{0}|\mathbf{x}_{\text{i}}\rangle.

In the
N\rightarrow\infty
limit, the amplitude (
6.17
)
can be put into the form,

\displaystyle K_{\text{fi}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int[D\mathbf{x}][D\mathbf{p}_{c}]U(\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{f}},\mathbf{p}_{\mathrm{f}}^{c})P\exp\left\{i\int_{t_{\text{i}}}^{t_{\text{f}}}dt\left[\mathbf{p}\cdot\dot{\mathbf{x}}+e\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\mathbf{x}\right.\right.

(6.18)

\displaystyle\left.\left.-\sigma_{3}\epsilon(\mathbf{p})-e\phi(\mathbf{x})-\bm{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{p})\cdot\dot{\mathbf{p}}\right]\right\}U^{\dagger}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{i}},\mathbf{p}_{\mathrm{i}}^{c}),

where we have used the following approximation,

\displaystyle U^{\dagger}(\mathbf{x}_{j},\mathbf{p}_{j}^{c})U(\mathbf{x}_{j},\mathbf{p}_{j-1}^{c})

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\exp\left[-i\bm{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{x}_{j},\mathbf{p}_{j-1}^{c})\cdot(\mathbf{p}_{j}^{c}-\mathbf{p}_{j-1}^{c})\right],

\displaystyle U^{\dagger}(\mathbf{x}_{j+1},\mathbf{p}_{j}^{c})U(\mathbf{x}_{j},\mathbf{p}_{j}^{c})

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\exp\left[i\bm{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{x}_{j},\mathbf{p}_{j}^{c})\cdot(e\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}_{j+1})-e\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}_{j}))\right],

(6.19)

and defined the Berry potential,

\displaystyle\bm{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle\equiv-iU_{\mathbf{p}}^{\dagger}\bm{\nabla}_{\mathbf{p}}U_{\mathbf{p}}=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2|\mathbf{p}|}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbf{e}_{\phi}\cot\frac{\theta}{2}&\mathbf{e}_{\phi}-i\mathbf{e}_{\theta}\\
\mathbf{e}_{\phi}+i\mathbf{e}_{\theta}&\mathbf{e}_{\phi}\tan\frac{\theta}{2}\end{array}\right).

With the amplitude in a general form,

K_{\mathrm{fi}}=\int[D\mathbf{x}][D\mathbf{p}]\exp(iS),

(6.23)

one can read out the effective action for the right-handed particle
from Eq. (
6.18
),

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dt\left[\mathbf{p}\cdot\dot{\mathbf{x}}+e\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\mathbf{x}-\sigma_{3}\epsilon(\mathbf{p})-e\phi(\mathbf{x})-\bm{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{p})\cdot\dot{\mathbf{p}}\right].

(6.24)

Here we assume that there is no transition between the particle and
anti-particle states during the adiabatic evolution, so one can neglect
the off-diagonal terms in Eq. (
6.24
). More details can
be found in Ref.
[
146
,
148
]
or in the appendix
of Ref.
[
147
]
.

Then the diagonal terms of Eq. (
6.24
) have the form
[
146
]
,

S_{\pm}=\int dt\left[\mathbf{p}\cdot\dot{\mathbf{x}}+e\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\mathbf{x}\mp\epsilon(\mathbf{p})-e\phi(\mathbf{x})-\mathbf{a}_{\pm}(\mathbf{p})\cdot\dot{\mathbf{p}}\right],

(6.25)

where
\mathbf{a}_{\pm}(\mathbf{p})=\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{11/22}(\mathbf{p})

is the Berry connection or potential for particles (upper) and anti-particles
(lower). More details can be found in Ref.
[
147
]
.
In general, there are quantum corrections to particle’s energy
\epsilon(\mathbf{p})

due to the Zeeman effects
[
145
,
147
]
. More general expression for
\epsilon(\mathbf{p})
can be found in Sec.

6.3
and
6.5
. Later, we will show that this quantum corrections are essential to get the Lorentz invariance in Sec.
6.4
.

Using the Euler-Lagrangian equations, we obtain the equations of motion
for
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{p}
,

\displaystyle\sqrt{G}\mathbf{\dot{x}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{p}}+\mathbf{E}\times\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{p}}+\mathbf{B}(\widehat{\mathbf{p}}\cdot\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{p}}),

\displaystyle\sqrt{G}\dot{\mathbf{p}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}+\widehat{\mathbf{p}}\times\mathbf{B}+\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{E}\cdot\mathbf{B}),

(6.26)

where
\widehat{\mathbf{p}}\equiv\mathbf{p}/|\mathbf{p}|
,
\sqrt{G}=1+\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{p}}\cdot\mathbf{B}

with

\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{p}}=\pm\frac{\mathbf{p}}{2|\mathbf{p}|^{3}},

(6.27)

being the Berry curvature for the right-handed particle (upper sign)
and anti-particle (lower sign). The first term for
\sqrt{G}\mathbf{\dot{x}}

is the standard velocity of a single particle and
\mathbf{E}+\widehat{\mathbf{p}}\times\mathbf{B}

in
\sqrt{G}\dot{\mathbf{p}}
is the Lorentz force. The new terms
proportional to
\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{p}}
are quantum corrections
to the effective velocity
\mathbf{\dot{x}}
and force
\dot{\mathbf{p}}
.
An important feature for the Berry curvature is

\bm{\nabla}_{\mathbf{p}}\cdot\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{p}}=2\pi\delta^{3}(\mathbf{p}),

(6.28)

which we will show later leads to the chiral anomaly.

Once one has the equations of motion (
6.26
) for single
particles, it is straightforward to derive the kinetic equation by
introducing the distribution function
f(t,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p})

representing the probability to find particles (the particle number
density) in phase space.

In a non-interacting system of fermions without collisions, the total
time derivative of the distribution function should vanish, so we have

\left[\sqrt{G}\partial_{t}+\sqrt{G}\dot{\mathbf{x}}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}+\sqrt{G}\dot{\mathbf{p}}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{p}}\right]f(t,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p})=0.

(6.29)

Here we have multiplied
\sqrt{G}
to the original equation. The
above equation is usually called the chiral kinetic equation for massless
fermions.

Integrating Eq. (
6.29
) over
\mathbf{p}
,
we obtain the continuity equation for the particle number of right-handed
fermions (chiral charge),

\partial_{t}\rho+\bm{\nabla}\cdot\mathbf{J}=\frac{1}{2\pi}(\mathbf{E}\cdot\mathbf{B})f(\mathbf{p}=0),

(6.30)

where
\rho
and
\mathbf{J}
are the particle number density and
current density,

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\sqrt{G}f(t,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\sqrt{G}\dot{\mathbf{x}}f(t,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p}),

(6.31)

and, we have also used the Maxwell equations and

\partial_{t}\sqrt{G}+\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\cdot(\sqrt{G}\dot{\mathbf{x}})+\nabla_{\mathbf{p}}\cdot(\sqrt{G}\dot{\mathbf{p}})=2\pi(\mathbf{E}\cdot\mathbf{B})\delta^{3}(\mathbf{p}).

(6.32)

Equation (
6.30
) means that the total chiral charge
is no longer conserved but can be changed by a source
2\pi(\mathbf{E}\cdot\mathbf{B})\delta^{3}(\mathbf{p})

coming from the Berry curvature (
6.28
). It is
remarkable that the Berry curvature plays a crucial role to reproduce
the chiral anomaly.

We assume that
f(t,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p})
is the Fermi-Dirac distribution
function, then we can compute the current and the right-hand side
of Eq. (
6.30
). Inserting Eq. (
6.26
) into
the current (
6.31
), it gives the chiral magnetic
effect for right-handed fermions,

\mathbf{J}_{R}=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\mu_{R}\mathbf{B},

(6.33)

and the continuity equation for the chiral charge with chiral anomaly,

\partial_{t}\rho_{R}+\bm{\nabla}\cdot\mathbf{J}_{R}=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}(\mathbf{E}\cdot\mathbf{B}),

(6.34)

where
\mu_{R}
is the chemical potential for right-handed fermions.

Although the chiral anomaly is a topological effect and should be intact by the interaction, it seems that the correct chiral anomaly
coefficient depends on the value
f(\mathbf{p}=\mathbf{0})
as shown in Eq. (
6.30
). One may wonder what happens if
f(\mathbf{p}=\mathbf{0})\neq 1

in off-equilibrium systems. Such a problem has been clarified in Refs.

[
372
,
373
]
in the covariant Wigner function approach.
It has been shown that the chiral anomaly is actually from the Dirac
sea or the vacuum contribution in the Wigner function without normal-ordering,
and this contribution modifies the chiral kinetic equation for antiparticles

[
372
,
373
]
.

The path integral approaches for the non-Abelian Berry phases are discussed in
Ref.
[
147
]
and also see Ref.
[
374
]
for the lattice QCD simulations of non-Abelian Berry curvatures.

6.3  Hamiltonian approach and symplectic form

In this subsection, we follow
Ref.
[
144
,
150
]
to illustrate the Hamiltonian
approach from the action (
6.25
).

In a general case, the particle might be dressed, e.g., in a condensed
matter system, we, therefore, extend the action (
6.25
)
to
[
163
,
375
]

S=\int dt\left[\mathbf{p}\cdot\dot{\mathbf{x}}+e\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{p}}\cdot\dot{\mathbf{p}}-H(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p})\right],

(6.35)

where
H(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p})
contains
\epsilon(\mathbf{p})

and
\phi(\mathbf{x})
. In general the particle’s energy can depend
on both
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{p}
. For simplicity, here we only
consider the action for particles (similar for anti-particles).

We introduce a
6
dimensional parameter
\xi^{a}=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p})

in phase space and the action (
6.35
) can be cast into

S=\int dt\left[-w_{a}\dot{\xi}^{a}-H(\xi)\right],

(6.36)

where
w_{a}=(-\dot{\mathbf{p}}-\dot{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{p}})
and lower index
a=1,2,...,6
.
The equations of motion or the Euler equations give

\displaystyle\omega_{ab}\dot{\xi}^{b}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial H}{\partial\xi^{a}},

(6.37)

with

\displaystyle\omega_{ab}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\partial_{a}w_{b}-\partial_{b}w_{a}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\epsilon_{ijk}\Omega_{k}&-\delta_{jk}\\
\delta_{jk}&\epsilon_{ijk}B_{k}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccc}0&\Omega_{3}&-\Omega_{2}&-1&0&0\\
-\Omega_{3}&0&\Omega_{1}&0&-1&0\\
\Omega_{2}&-\Omega_{1}&0&0&0&-1\\
1&0&0&0&-B_{3}&B_{2}\\
0&1&0&B_{3}&0&-B_{1}\\
0&0&1&-B_{2}&B_{1}&0\end{array}\right),

where
\bm{\Omega}
is the Berry curvature in Eq. (
6.27
).

The dynamics of the system by the action (
6.36
) is
defined in phase space and can be written in a symplectic form
[
375
]
.
Similar to the metric tensor
\eta_{\mu\nu}
,
\omega_{ab}
is called
the symplectic matrix and it is anti-symmetric with its inverse
\omega^{ab}=(\omega^{-1})^{ab}

satisfying

\omega^{ab}\omega_{bc}=\delta_{c}^{a}.

(6.48)

The invariant volume in this symplectic form is given by

dV=\frac{1}{d!}\omega^{d}=\sqrt{\det\omega_{ab}}\prod_{\alpha=1}^{2d}d\xi^{\alpha},

(6.49)

where

\det\omega_{ab}=(1+\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{p}}\cdot\mathbf{B})^{2}.

(6.50)

Therefore the invariant phase space volume becomes
\sqrt{G}d^{3}\mathbf{x}d^{3}\mathbf{p}
.
Similar to
\eta_{\mu\nu}
, we also have

\partial_{b}(\sqrt{\det(\omega_{ab})}\;\omega^{ab})=0.

(6.51)

We also introduce the Poisson bracket in a symplectic form related
to
\omega_{ab}
,

\{f,g\}_{\omega}=\omega^{ab}\partial_{a}f\partial_{b}g.

(6.52)

Equation (
6.37
) can be put into the form,

\dot{\xi}^{a}=\{H,\xi^{a}\}_{\omega}=\{\xi^{b},\xi^{a}\}_{\omega}\frac{\partial H}{\partial\xi^{b}}.

(6.53)

The following Poisson brackets are given by
[
375
,
144
]

\displaystyle\{\mathbf{p}_{i},\mathbf{p}_{j}\}_{\omega}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\epsilon_{ijk}B_{k}}{1+\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{p}}\cdot\mathbf{B}},

\displaystyle\{\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{x}_{j}\}_{\omega}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon_{ijk}\Omega_{k}}{1+\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{p}}\cdot\mathbf{B}},

\displaystyle\{\mathbf{p}_{i},\mathbf{x}_{j}\}_{\omega}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta_{ij}+\Omega_{i}B_{j}}{1+\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{p}}\cdot\mathbf{B}},

(6.54)

which are quite different from conventional Poisson brackets for
x

and
p
in classical mechanics. The structure of the phase space
in the presence of the Berry curvature becomes more complicated.

Our aim is to compute the time evolution of the occupation number
density operator
n_{\mathbf{p}}
through the Heisenberg equation,

\partial_{t}n_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{x})=i[H,n_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{x})].

(6.55)

In a usual quantum system, we can assume that two operators
\widehat{A}_{1}

and
\widehat{A}_{2}
are linear in
n_{\mathbf{p}}
,

\widehat{A}_{i}=\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}d^{3}\mathbf{x}}{(2\pi)^{3}}A_{i}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p})n_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{x}),

(6.56)

where
A_{i}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p})
for
i=1,2
are phase space
functions. Then their commutator is given by

[\widehat{A}_{1},\widehat{A}_{2}]=-i\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}d^{3}\mathbf{x}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\left\{\frac{\partial A_{1}}{\partial\mathbf{p}}\cdot\frac{\partial A_{2}}{\partial\mathbf{x}}-\frac{\partial A_{1}}{\partial\mathbf{x}}\cdot\frac{\partial A_{2}}{\partial\mathbf{p}}\right\}n_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{x}),

(6.57)

where
we have used

[n_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{x}),n_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{y})]=-i(2\pi)^{3}[\nabla_{\mathbf{p}}\delta(p-q)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\delta(x-y)][n_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{y})-n_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})].

(6.58)

In the system with action (
6.35
), we need to revisit
the above calculation. In the symplectic manifold, the operators
\widehat{A}_{i}

now become

\widehat{A}_{i}=\int d\xi\sqrt{\det\omega_{ab}}A_{i}(\xi)n(\xi),

(6.59)

where
n(\xi)
is the occupation number density operator in phase
space of
\xi
. The bracket term
\{...\}
in the left handed sided of Eq. (
6.57
),
which is the classical Poisson brackets,
is changed to the one in Eq. (
6.52
). It is
natural to define the commutator (
6.57
) as
[
144
]

[\widehat{A}_{1},\widehat{A}_{2}]=-i\int d\xi\sqrt{\det\omega_{ab}}\{A_{1},A_{2}\}_{\omega}n_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{x}).

(6.60)

Usually, the above description is implemented to the low energy or
low-temperature systems, e.g., Fermi liquid at the low temperature,
in which the physics near the Fermi surface is relevant. Integrating
by part and using Eq. (
6.51
), one
can rewrite Eq. (
6.60
) as

[\widehat{A}_{1},\widehat{A}_{2}]=-\frac{i}{2}\int d\xi\sqrt{\det\omega_{cd}}\omega^{ba}(A_{1}\partial_{b}A_{2}-A_{2}\partial_{b}A_{1})\partial_{a}n(\xi).

(6.61)

If
\widehat{A}_{i}
are general functions of
n(\xi)
, one can
also generalize the above commutator as

[\widehat{A}_{1},\widehat{A}_{2}]=-\frac{i}{2}\int d\xi\sqrt{\det\omega_{cd}}\omega^{ba}\left(\frac{\delta A_{1}}{\delta n(\xi)}\partial_{b}\frac{\delta A_{2}}{\delta n(\xi)}-\frac{\delta A_{2}}{\delta n(\xi)}\partial_{b}\frac{\delta A_{1}}{\delta n(\xi)}\right)\partial_{a}n(\xi).

(6.62)

Now we implement Eq. (
6.61
) to compute the commutator
of two operators for the particle number density,

\displaystyle[\rho(\mathbf{y}),\rho(\mathbf{z})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{2}\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}d^{3}\mathbf{x}}{(2\pi)^{3}}(1+\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{p}}\cdot\mathbf{B})

(6.63)

\displaystyle\times[\delta(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\partial_{i}^{x}\delta(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z})-\delta(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z})\partial_{i}^{x}\delta(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})]

\displaystyle\times\left[\{x^{i},x^{j}\}_{\omega}\frac{\partial n_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{x})}{\partial x^{j}}+\{x^{i},p^{j}\}_{\omega}\frac{\partial n_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{x})}{\partial p^{j}}\right],

where we have used the relation for the particle number density operator,

\displaystyle\rho(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}n_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{x})\sqrt{\det\omega_{ab}}

(6.64)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}d^{3}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{3}}(1+\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{p}}\cdot\mathbf{B})\delta(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})n_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}).

Using the relations (
6.54
), we can compute
each term in Eq. (
6.63
). For the terms proportional
to
\{x^{i},x^{j}\}_{\omega}
, we find

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}d^{3}\mathbf{x}}{(2\pi)^{3}}(1+\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{p}}\cdot\mathbf{B})\delta(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\partial_{i}^{x}\delta(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z})\{x^{i},x^{j}\}_{\omega}\frac{\partial n_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{x})}{\partial x^{j}}

(6.65)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\left\{\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}\times\bm{\sigma}(\mathbf{y})\right\}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}\delta(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{z}).

where

\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{x})\equiv-\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}p_{i}\Omega_{k}\frac{\partial n_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{x})}{\partial p^{k}}.

(6.66)

While the terms proportional to
\{x^{i},p^{j}\}_{\omega}
can be
calculated as

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}d^{3}\mathbf{x}}{(2\pi)^{3}}(1+\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{p}}\cdot\mathbf{B})\delta(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\partial_{i}^{x}\delta(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z})\{x^{i},p^{j}\}_{\omega}\frac{\partial n_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{x})}{\partial p^{j}}

(6.67)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i[\mathbf{B}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}\delta(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{z})]\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}(\nabla_{\mathbf{p}}\cdot\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{p}})n_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{y}).

In reaching the above result for
I_{2}
, we have assumed that the
number density is just a Fermi sphere
n_{\mathbf{p}}^{\mathrm{F.S.}}(\mathbf{x})\propto\theta(|\mathbf{p}|-p_{F})

with
p_{F}
being the Fermi momentum, which is a good approximation
at low temperatures and high densities. Then we obtain

I_{2}=-\frac{\mathcal{K}}{8\pi^{2}}\mathbf{B}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}\delta(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{z}),

(6.68)

where
\mathcal{K}
is the monopole charge

\mathcal{K}\equiv\frac{1}{2\pi}\int d\mathbf{S}\cdot\bm{\Omega_{\mathbf{p}}}.

(6.69)

The commutator can then be put into a compact form,

[\rho(\mathbf{y}),\rho(\mathbf{z})]=-i\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}\times\bm{\sigma}(\mathbf{y})+\frac{\mathcal{K}}{4\pi^{2}}\mathbf{B}\right]\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}\delta(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{z}).

(6.70)

We now compute the evolution equation for
\rho(\mathbf{x})
. The
Hamiltonian
H
in the action (
6.35
) can be written
as

H=\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}d^{3}\mathbf{x}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\epsilon_{\mathbf{p}}\delta n_{\mathbf{p}}+\int d^{3}\mathbf{x}\phi(\mathbf{x})\rho(\mathbf{x}),

(6.71)

where
\epsilon_{\mathbf{p}}
is the energy of a single quasi-particle,

\delta n_{\mathbf{p}}=n_{\mathbf{p}}-n_{\mathbf{p}}^{\textrm{F.S.}}

is the deviation from the ground state
n_{\mathbf{p}}^{\textrm{F.S.}}
,
and
\phi(\mathbf{x})
is the electric potential of the electromagnetic
field appearing in Eq. (
6.9
). In general, the interaction
can change
\epsilon_{\mathbf{p}}
, one can also introduce an extra
term,

\frac{1}{2}\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}d^{3}\mathbf{q}d^{3}\mathbf{x}}{(2\pi)^{6}}g(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})\delta n_{\mathbf{p}}\delta n_{\mathbf{q}},

(6.72)

to account for the modification from the interaction with the Landau
parameter
g(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})
. Using Eqs. (
6.62
) and
(
6.70
), we find

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i[H,\rho(\mathbf{x})]=-\nabla\cdot\mathbf{j}+\frac{\mathcal{K}}{4\pi^{2}}\mathbf{B}\cdot\mathbf{E},

(6.73)

where we have used
\mathbf{E}=-\nabla\phi(\mathbf{x})
.

Equation (
6.73
) is the same as Eq. (
6.34
).
The current is

\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{x})=\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\left[\frac{\partial\epsilon_{\mathbf{p}}}{\partial\mathbf{p}}n_{\mathbf{p}}-\left(\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{p}}\cdot\frac{\partial n_{\mathbf{p}}}{\partial\mathbf{p}}\right)\epsilon_{\mathbf{p}}\mathbf{B}-\epsilon_{\mathbf{p}}\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{p}}\times\frac{\partial n_{\mathbf{p}}}{\partial\mathbf{x}}\right]+\mathbf{E}\times\bm{\sigma}(\mathbf{x}),

(6.74)

where
\partial\epsilon_{\mathbf{p}}/\partial\mathbf{p}
is the particle’s
velocity, the second term proportional to
\mathbf{B}
leads to the
chiral magnetic currents, and the last term
\mathbf{E}\times\bm{\sigma}(\mathbf{x})

corresponds to
\mathbf{E}\times\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{p}}

in Eq. (
6.26
). We see that the term
\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{p}}\times(\partial n_{\mathbf{p}}/\partial\mathbf{x})

implies that an inhomogeneous distribution function can also lead
to a current. Recently, such a term provides new sources for the local
spin polarization, e.g., the polarization induced by the shear viscous
term and gradient of the chemical potential over temperature
[
173
,
300
,
297
,
301
,
298
,
282
]
.

6.4  Lorentz transformation and side jump

We consider the Lorentz covariance of the chiral kinetic equation
in this subsection. Restoring the Planck constant, the action (
6.25
)
for the particle is

S=\int\left[(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{A})\cdot d\mathbf{x}-\left(|\mathbf{p}|-\hbar\frac{\mathbf{B}\cdot\mathbf{p}}{2|\mathbf{p}|^{2}}+\phi\right)dt-\hbar\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{p}}\cdot d\mathbf{p}\right],

(6.75)

where
\mathbf{B}\cdot\mathbf{p}/(2|\mathbf{p}|^{2})
is the correction
to the particle’s energy from the magnetic fields, i.e. Zeeman effect
for massless fermions.

At
O(\hbar^{0})
, it is straightforward to verify that the action
is invariant under the standard infinitesimal Lorentz transformation

[
148
]
,

\displaystyle\delta\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{\beta}_{u}t,

\displaystyle\delta t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{\beta}_{u}\cdot\mathbf{x},

\displaystyle\delta\mathbf{p}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{\beta}_{u}|\mathbf{p}|,

\displaystyle\delta\mathbf{B}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{\beta}_{u}\times\mathbf{E},

\displaystyle\delta\mathbf{E}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\bm{\beta}_{u}\times\mathbf{B},

(6.76)

where
\bm{\beta}_{u}
is the boost velocity. At
O(\hbar^{1})
,
under the transformation (
6.76
) we have

\displaystyle\delta\left(\frac{\mathbf{B}\cdot\mathbf{p}}{2|\mathbf{p}|^{2}}dt\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[-\frac{(\bm{\beta}_{u}\times\mathbf{E})\cdot\mathbf{p}}{2|\mathbf{p}|^{2}}-\frac{\mathbf{B}\cdot\bm{\beta}_{u}}{2|\mathbf{p}|}-\frac{(\mathbf{B}\cdot\mathbf{p})(\bm{\beta}_{u}\cdot\mathbf{p})}{|\mathbf{p}|^{3}}\right]dt

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathbf{B}\cdot\mathbf{p}}{2|\mathbf{p}|^{2}}(\bm{\beta}_{u}\cdot d\mathbf{x}),

\displaystyle\delta(\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{p}}\cdot d\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\mathbf{p}|(\bm{\beta}_{u}\times\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{p}})\cdot d\mathbf{p}-d(\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{p}}\cdot\bm{\beta}_{u}|\mathbf{p}|),

(6.77)

which lead to the variation of the action,

\displaystyle\delta S

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\hbar\int\left[\frac{(\bm{\beta}_{u}\times\mathbf{p})}{2|\mathbf{p}|^{2}}\cdot(d\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{E}-\widehat{\mathbf{p}}\times\mathbf{B})-\frac{(\mathbf{B}\cdot\mathbf{p})(\bm{\beta}_{u}\cdot\mathbf{p})}{2|\mathbf{p}|^{3}}\right]dt

(6.78)

\displaystyle+\hbar\int\left[\frac{\mathbf{B}\cdot\mathbf{p}}{2|\mathbf{p}|^{2}}(\bm{\beta}_{u}\cdot d\mathbf{x})dt\right].

Here we have dropped the total derivative term
+\hbar\int d\left(\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{p}}\cdot\bm{\beta}|\mathbf{p}|\right)
.
It is obvious that
\delta S
is non-vanishing in general. The
standard Lorentz symmetry seems to be broken.

There are two ways to solve this problem. One way is to modify the
standard infinitesimal Lorentz transformation (
6.76
),
e.g., add quantum corrections to Eq. (
6.76
) to cancel

\delta S
in Eq. (
6.78
). The second way is more
rigorous. We emphasize that in quantum field theory the Lorentz transformation
is well-defined and should not be modified. In the Wigner function
approach, we will demonstrate that there are quantum corrections to
the distribution function under the Lorentz transformation
[
151
]
.
We will discuss it later in this subsection.

In the first way, we assume the modified Lorentz transformation to
the order of
\hbar

[
148
]
,

\delta\mathbf{x}=\bm{\beta}_{u}t+\hbar\mathbf{G},\;\delta\mathbf{p}=\bm{\beta}_{u}|\mathbf{p}|+\hbar\mathbf{F},

(6.79)

then we have

\displaystyle\delta[(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{A})\cdot d\mathbf{x}-(|\mathbf{p}|+\phi)dt]

(6.80)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathbf{F}\cdot d\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{p}\cdot d\mathbf{G}+\frac{\mathbf{F}\cdot\mathbf{p}}{2|\mathbf{p}|}dt+(\mathbf{G}\times\mathbf{B})\cdot d\mathbf{x}+(\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{E})dt.

Compared with Eq. (
6.78
), we obtain

\displaystyle\delta\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{\beta}_{u}t+\hbar\frac{\bm{\beta}_{u}\times\widehat{\mathbf{p}}}{2|\mathbf{p}|},

\displaystyle\delta\mathbf{p}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{\beta}_{u}|\mathbf{p}|-\hbar\frac{\mathbf{B}\cdot\mathbf{p}}{2|\mathbf{p}|^{2}}+\hbar\frac{\bm{\beta}_{u}\times\widehat{\mathbf{p}}}{2|\mathbf{p}|}\times\mathbf{B},

(6.81)

which is called the side-jump
[
376
]
.

Figure 6.1:
Illustration of the side jump and total angular momentum conservation
for massless fermions. (a) Two right-handed fermions collide with
each other. The initial and final momenta of fermions are assumed
to be along
z
and
y
directions, respectively. The orbital angular
momentum and spin are conserved independently in this special case.
(b) We boost the system along the
z
direction. The spins in the
final state do not cancel. (c) To keep the total angular momentum
conserved, there are quantum corrections to the position of the final
state.

The origin of the side jump effect comes from the total angular momentum
conservation. To illustrate it, we can consider a collision of two
right-handed fermions in the absence of background electromagnetic fields
as shown in Fig.
6.1
. In Fig.
6.1
(a),
we simply assume that the initial and final fermions move along the

z
and
y
direction, respectively. We see that that the (classical)
orbital angular momentum and spin are conserved separately. Then we
make a Lorentz boost along the
z
direction as shown in Fig.
6.1
(b).
The total orbital angular momentum and spin in the initial state are
vanishing separately. In the final state, the total orbital angular
momentum is not modified. Due to the change of final momenta, the
total spin in the final state becomes nonzero. In order to make the
total angular momentum conserve, the only way in this scenario is
to introduce additional corrections to the orbital angular momentum
to cancel such a change of the total spin. That is the reason for
the extra quantum correction to
\delta\mathbf{x}
in Eq. (
6.81
).

The above discussion can also be extended to the finite Lorentz transformation

[
149
]
. For massless fermions, the total angular momentum
tensor is in a canonical form,

J^{\mu\nu}=x^{\mu}p^{\nu}-x^{\nu}p^{\mu}+S^{\mu\nu},

(6.82)

where
S^{\mu\nu}
is usually called the canonical spin. In general,
one can also introduce another decomposition of
J^{\mu\nu}
through
the pseudo-gauge transformation. One can always make the following transformation
for
x^{\mu}
and
S^{\mu\nu}
,

\displaystyle x^{\mu}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle x^{\prime\mu}=x^{\mu}+\delta x^{\mu},

\displaystyle S^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle S^{\prime\mu\nu}=S^{\mu\nu}+\delta x^{\nu}p^{\mu}-\delta x^{\mu}p^{\nu},

(6.83)

that keeps
J^{\mu\nu}
invariant. One can find more detailed discussion
in recent reviews
[
89
,
310
]
and related
works for dissipative spin hydrodynamics
[
309
,
311
,
312
,
332
,
323
]
.

As a spin tensor,
S^{\mu\nu}
has 6 degrees of freedom, while the
spin of the massless fermion is always parallel or anti-parallel to
its momentum. To remove non-physical degrees of freedom, one needs
to impose an additional condition, Frenkel-Weyssenhoff condition,

p_{\mu}S^{\mu\nu}=0.

(6.84)

We find that Eq. (
6.84
) only requires
p\cdot\delta x=0

under the transformation (
6.83
), which means that
the condition (
6.84
) is insufficient to fix
S^{\mu\nu}
.
Another condition is needed, and one can choose

n^{\mu}S_{\mu\nu}=0,

(6.85)

where
n^{\mu}
is the frame vector or velocity of an arbitrary frame.
These two conditions, (
6.84
) and (
6.85
), indicate the
following form of
S^{\mu\nu}
,

S_{(n)}^{\mu\nu}=\lambda\frac{\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}p_{\alpha}n_{\beta}}{2(p\cdot n)},

(6.86)

where
\lambda=\pm
denotes the helicity for the right- (upper sign)
and left-handed (lower sign) fermions, and the subscript
(n)
stands
for the frame vector
n^{\mu}
. There are two common choices for

n^{\mu}
: the local rest frame
(1,\bm{0})
and the fluid
velocity
u^{\mu}
.

Now we consider the finite Lorentz transformation for the coordinate

x^{\mu}
. Under the transformation from the frame
n^{\mu}
to

n^{\prime\mu}
, using Eq. (
6.83
), we obtain

S_{(n^{\prime})}^{\prime\mu\nu}-S_{(n)}^{\mu\nu}=\delta x_{(nn^{\prime})}^{\nu}p^{\mu}-\delta x_{(nn^{\prime})}^{\mu}p^{\nu},

(6.87)

which leads to
[
377
,
166
,
149
]

\delta x_{(nn^{\prime})}^{\mu}=\lambda\frac{\epsilon^{\mu\alpha\beta\gamma}p_{\alpha}n_{\beta}n_{\gamma}^{\prime}}{2(p\cdot n)(p\cdot n^{\prime})}.

(6.88)

Here the subscript
(nn^{\prime})
indicates the frame dependence
of
\delta x^{\mu}
. In deriving the above equation, we have imposed
another condition
\delta x_{(nn^{\prime})}\cdot n=\delta x_{(nn^{\prime})}\cdot n^{\prime}=0
.
Equation (
6.88
) is an extension of Eq. (
6.81
).

In relativistic kinetic theory, the distribution function
f(t,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p})

is not a scalar under Lorentz transformation as expected. Due to the
quantum corrections to both
x
and
p
, we need to look at
f(x,p)

in two different frames
n
and
n^{\prime}
. After a rigorous derivation
of both
\delta x^{\mu}
and
\delta p^{\mu}
in finite Lorentz
transformation in quantum field theory, we find

\displaystyle x^{\mu}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle x^{\prime\mu}=x^{\mu}+\hbar N_{(nn^{\prime})}^{\mu},

\displaystyle p^{\mu}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle p^{\prime\mu}=p^{\mu}+\hbar N_{\nu}^{(nn^{\prime})}F^{\mu\nu},

(6.89)

where
N_{(nn^{\prime})}^{\mu}
is just
\delta x_{(nn^{\prime})}^{\mu}
.
Then
f_{(n^{\prime})}^{\prime}(x^{\prime},p^{\prime})
in
n^{\prime}

frame is given by
[
151
]

f_{(n^{\prime})}^{\prime}(x^{\prime},p^{\prime})=f_{(n)}(x,p)+\hbar N_{(nn^{\prime})}^{\mu}[\partial_{\mu}^{x}+F_{\nu\mu}\partial_{p}^{\nu}]f_{(n)}(x,p).

(6.90)

To keep a relativistic vector
\mathscr{J}^{\mu}(x,p)
in phase space
(vector component of the Wigner function for fermions with a fixed
chirality, see Secs
4
and
4.2
)
covariant, we should have

\mathscr{J}^{\mu}(x,p)\sim p^{\mu}f+S_{(n)}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}f,

(6.91)

in absence of background electromagnetic fields. In a three-vector
form, the above current becomes

\mathbf{J}=\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\left(\widehat{\mathbf{p}}-\frac{\mathbf{p}}{2|\mathbf{p}|^{3}}\times\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\right)f(t,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p}),

(6.92)

where the second term related to
S_{(n)}^{\mu\nu}
corresponds to
the last term in the bracket in Eq. (
6.74
). Such a
term can also be understood as the curl of the magnetization
\mathbf{M}
,

\mathbf{M}=\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{\mathbf{p}}{2|\mathbf{p}|^{3}}f(t,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p}).

(6.93)

Such a contribution to the current from the magnetization is crucial
to reproduce the correct transport coefficient of the CVE
[
148
]
, also see Ref.
[
371
]

for the discussion of similar terms for the CME
at finite frequencies. A systematic derivation of the current and
more discussion about the frame dependence are given in Sec.
5.4
.

6.5  Effective theories

We briefly discuss in this subsection several other approaches to
the chiral kinetic equation from effective theories
[
145
,
160
,
156
,
157
,
158
,
161
]
.
The starting point is to derive an effective Lagrangian through integrating
out irrelevant modes and obtain the equations of motion for the two-point
Green functions. One finally obtains the effective evolution equations
for distribution functions encoded in the two-point Green functions.

One convenient way to study chiral fermions is through the high-density
effective theory
[
145
]
(also see the Refs.
[
378
,
379
,
380
]
for the frameworks of the high-density effective theory). To extend the effective theories beyond the zero temperature limit, the authors in Refs.
[
156
,
157
,
158
,
161
]
have
developed the on-shell effective theory (OSEFT), which can recover the
results derived from high-density effective theories in the zero temperature limits.
Moreover, it has been pointed out by Ref.
[
158
]
that both high-density and on-shell effective theories are the counter-part of a Foldy-Wouthuysen diagonalization
[
381
]
,
in which the particles are antiparticles are disentangled after redefining.

For simplicity,
in this subsection, we only concentrate on the high-density effective theory.
In this theory, one concentrates on the physics near the Fermi surface.
The momentum of a particle can be written as

p^{\mu}=\mu v^{\mu}+l^{\mu},

(6.94)

where
\mu
is the chemical potential for right-handed fermions,

v^{\mu}=(1,\mathbf{v}),

\overline{v}^{\mu}=(1,-\mathbf{v})
with

\mathbf{v}
being a unit three-vector, and
l^{\mu}=(l^{0},\mathbf{l})

satisfying
|\mathbf{l}|\ll\mu
. The spinor can then be decomposed
as

\displaystyle\psi(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}e^{-ip\cdot x}\psi(p)=\sum_{v}e^{i\mu{\bf v}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\psi_{v}(x)=\sum_{v}e^{i\mu\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{x}}[\psi_{+v}(x)+\psi_{-v}(x)],

(6.95)

where

\psi_{\pm v}(x)\equiv P_{\pm}(\mathbf{v})\psi_{v}(x)=\int_{|l|<\mu}\frac{d^{4}l}{(2\pi)^{4}}e^{-il\cdot x}\psi_{\pm v}(l),

(6.96)

with the projector
P_{\pm}(\mathbf{v})=(1/2)(1\pm\bm{\sigma}\cdot\mathbf{v})
,
and
\psi_{\pm v}
stands for the slow and fast modes, respectively.
Applying Eq. (
6.95
) to the standard Lagrangian of
the right-handed fermions,

\mathcal{L}=\psi^{\dagger}(i\sigma^{\mu}D_{\mu}+\mu)\psi,

(6.97)

and expressing fast modes by slow modes via the equations of motion,

(2\mu+i\overline{v}\cdot D)\psi_{-v}+i\sigma\cdot D_{\perp}\psi_{+v}=0,

(6.98)

we obtain the effective Lagrangian,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\textrm{EFT}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{v}\psi_{+v}^{\dagger}iv\cdot D\psi_{+v}(x)+\psi_{+v}^{\dagger}\sigma\cdot D_{\perp}\frac{1}{2\mu+i\overline{v}\cdot D}\sigma\cdot D_{\perp}\psi_{+v},

(6.99)

where
D_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}+iA_{\mu}
and
\textcolor
blue
D_{\perp}^{\mu}=D^{\mu}-(v\cdot D)v^{\mu}
.
In the large
\mu
limit, we can expand
\mathcal{L}_{\textrm{EFT}}

in the power series of
1/\mu
,

\mathcal{L}_{\textrm{EFT}}=\sum_{n}\mathcal{L}^{(n)},\;\mathcal{L}^{(n)}=\sum_{v}\psi_{+v}^{\dagger}\mathcal{D}^{(n)}\psi_{+v},

(6.100)

where the effective operators are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle iv\cdot D,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(\sigma\cdot D_{\perp})^{2}}{2\mu},

\displaystyle D^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{4\mu^{2}}(\sigma\cdot D^{\perp})(\overline{v}\cdot D)(\sigma\cdot D^{\perp}).

(6.101)

The explicit form of
\mathcal{L}^{(1)}
at
O(1/\mu)
is

\mathcal{L}^{(1)}=\psi_{+v}^{\dagger}\left[\frac{D_{\perp}^{2}+\mathbf{B}\cdot\bm{\sigma}}{2\mu}\right]\psi_{+v}=\psi_{+v}^{\dagger}\left[\frac{D_{\perp}^{2}+\mathbf{B}\cdot\mathbf{v}}{2\mu}\right]\psi_{+v}.

(6.102)

In order to derive the currents, we need to compute the effective
theory up to
O(1/\mu^{2})
. We introduce the two-point function

G_{v}
,

G_{v}(x_{1},x_{2})\equiv\left\langle\psi_{+v}(x_{1})\psi_{+v}^{\dagger}(x_{2})\right\rangle,

(6.103)

which satisfies the equations of motion,

\mathcal{D}_{x1}G_{v}(x_{1},x_{2})=0,\;G_{v}(x_{1},x_{2})\mathcal{D}_{x2}^{\dagger}=0,

(6.104)

under the condition,

P_{-}G_{v}(x_{1},x_{2})=G_{v}(x_{1},x_{2})P_{-}=0,

(6.105)

where
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}^{(0)}+\mathcal{D}^{(1)}+\mathcal{D}^{(2)}+O(1/\mu^{3})
.

We define

x_{1}=x+\frac{y}{2},\;x_{2}=x-\frac{y}{2},

(6.106)

and consider the gradient expansion under the following power counting,

\displaystyle\partial_{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(\epsilon_{1}),\;\partial_{s}=O(\epsilon_{2}),\;A_{\mu}=O(\epsilon_{3}),

\displaystyle\epsilon_{1}

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle\epsilon_{2,3}\ll 1,

(6.107)

where
\epsilon_{1,2,3}
are all small and positive numbers playing
as expansion parameters. For simplicity, we assume that the system
is homogeneous,

\partial_{x}^{\mu}F^{\alpha\beta}(x)=0,\;\partial_{i}^{x}f_{v}(x,l)=0,\;i=1,2,3,

(6.108)

where
f_{v}(x,l)
is the distribution function.

We now expand the equations of motion (
6.104
) order
by order and compute
G_{v}
. After a lengthy but straightforward
calculation, one finds
[
160
]

\displaystyle G_{v}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi P_{+}(\mathbf{v})\delta\left(l_{0}-l_{\parallel}-\frac{l_{\perp}^{2}-\mathbf{B}\cdot\mathbf{v}}{2\mu}+\frac{l_{\parallel}[l_{\perp}^{2}-(\mathbf{B}\cdot\mathbf{v})]}{2\mu^{2}}\right.\left.+\frac{\mathbf{B}\cdot\mathbf{l}_{\perp}+(\mathbf{E}\times\mathbf{l})\cdot\mathbf{v}}{4\mu^{2}}\right)f_{v}(x,l)+O(1/\mu^{3}),

(6.109)

and the particle’s energy,

\displaystyle\epsilon_{p}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle p^{0}=\mu+l^{0}=|\mathbf{p}|-\mathbf{B}\cdot\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{p}}}{2|\mathbf{p}|^{2}}+O(1/\mu^{3}),

(6.110)

where

\displaystyle\mathbf{l}_{\parallel}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{l})\mathbf{v},\;\mathbf{l}_{\perp}=\mathbf{l}-\mathbf{l}_{\parallel},

\displaystyle l_{\parallel}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle|\mathbf{l}_{\parallel}|=\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{l},\;l\equiv|\mathbf{l}|=\sqrt{l_{\parallel}^{2}+l_{\perp}^{2}}.

(6.111)

Note that, the particle’s energy
\epsilon_{p}
also depends on
\mathbf{v}
. When one simply chooses
\mathbf{l}\parallel\mathbf{v}
, the dispersion relation reduces to a simple form in Eq.(
6.110
)
[
160
]
.

Inserting Eq. (
6.109
) into Eq. (
6.104
) yields
the effective kinetic theory for the distribution function
f_{v}
.
We comment that the effective theory derived from the high density effective
theory looks different with Eq. (
6.32
). We emphasize that
f_{v}
is the distribution function for the particles dressed with anti-particles, while the standard
distribution function
f
describes distributions of the particles with positive energy only.
Fortunately, one can prove that the kinetic theory for
f_{v}
derived from the high density effective theory is equivalent to Eq. (
6.32
). More systematical discussion can be found in Ref.
[
160
]
.

Another method to derive the CKT is the on-shell effective field theory
[
156
,
157
,
158
,
161
]
,
in which the momentum of a fermion near its mass-shell can be written
as

p^{\mu}=p^{0}v^{\mu}+l^{\mu},\qquad\mathrm{for}\;|l|\ll p^{0},

(6.112)

similar to Eq. (
6.94
) in the high-density effective
theory. Instead of integrating out slow modes, one can integrate out
off-shell modes. The advantage of on-shell effective theories is that it can
be implemented to the finite temperature cases. Recently the collision terms have been derived in the on-shell effective theory
[
158
]
.

Another important problem in effective theories is the reparametrization invariance. This problem for CKT was first discussed in the on-shell effective theory
[
157
]
and then in high-density effective theory
[
160
]
.
For example, the
high-density effective theory is invariant under re-parametrization
in general,

v^{\mu}\rightarrow v^{\prime\mu}=v^{\mu}+\delta v^{\mu},\qquad l^{\mu}\rightarrow l^{\prime\mu}=l^{\mu}-\mu\delta v^{\mu},

(6.113)

where
p^{\mu}
is invariant and
v\cdot\delta v=0
. However, it
is found that the delta function for
l_{0}
in Eq. (
6.109
)
is not invariant under the transformation (
6.113
).
Although several works
[
157
,
160
]
have systematically discussed this problem, so far it is still an open question.

It is also possible to derive the chiral kinetic theory from the world-line
formalism
[
154
,
153
,
382
]
.
Note that the standard Schwinger proper time method or world-line
formalism provides the amplitude from the in-state to the out-state.
However, the evolution equations for particles require the expectation
value on the in-state
[
204
,
205
]
. Although
the CME has been computed from the real time world-line
formalism
[
204
,
205
]
, it is challenging
to derive the chiral kinetic theory in this formalism.
For the real-time simulations for the Schwinger mechanics from Wigner functions, one can see Refs.
[
383
,
384
,
385
,
386
,
387
]
and the references therein.

6.6  Discussions

A few remarks have to be made to end this section.

In the effective action (
6.25
), we have dropped off-diagonal
terms due to the assumption of adiabatic processes since they correspond
to transitions between states of different energies. However, the chiral
anomaly does not depend on this assumption. So a more rigorous way
free of such an assumption is to derive the chiral kinetic equation
from the quantum field theory, e.g., the covariant Wigner function
approach. The details of the covariant Wigner function approach to
the chiral kinetic equation are given in Sec.
5
.

The collision terms in the chiral effective equation are neglected,
see, e.g., Eq. (
6.29
). For transport phenomena,
particle collisions are essential and discussed in Sec.
7
and
10
.

7  Kinetic equations for massless fermions with collisions

In previous sections, we have not considered
interactions of massless fermions. This section will take them into
account and derive the Kadanoff-Baym equation with collisions for
massless fermions in background electromagnetic fields, from which
one can derive the chiral kinetic equation with collisions. Let us
start with the dressed propagator for right-handed fermions. In a
diagrammatic approach on the closed-time path in Fig.
2.1
,
the dressed propagator can be written as

S=S_{0}-iS_{0}\Sigma S=S_{0}-iS\Sigma S_{0},

(7.1)

where
\Sigma
is the self-energy, and
S_{0}
is the free propagator.
For brevity of the notation, we omitted all indices such as the ones
for spinors and the CTP. Note that here
S
does not necessarily
represent the lesser propagator taken in Sec.
3
.
Explicitly, the product is defined as

[AB]_{\alpha\beta}^{ab}(x_{1},x_{2})\equiv\sum_{\gamma}\int d^{4}z\left[A_{\alpha\gamma}^{a+}(x_{1},z)B_{\gamma\beta}^{+b}(z,x_{2})-A_{\alpha\gamma}^{a-}(x_{1},z)B_{\gamma\beta}^{-b}(z,x_{2})\right],

(7.2)

where
a,b\in\{+,-\}
denote the time branch on the CTP. Accordingly
the original integral is
\int_{C}=\int_{C^{+}}
and
\int_{C^{-}}
,
where
C^{+}
and
C^{-}
are the forward and backward paths respectively.
The minus sign for the last term is due to the backward path in Fig.
2.1
.
The lesser component of Eq. (
7.1
) involve the following
terms,

\displaystyle(S_{0}\Sigma S)^{<}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle-(S_{0}\Sigma S)^{+-}=-iS_{0}^{R}\Sigma^{R}S^{<}+iS_{0}^{R}\Sigma^{<}S^{A}-iS_{0}^{<}\Sigma^{A}S^{A},

\displaystyle(S\Sigma S_{0})^{<}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle-(S\Sigma S_{0})^{+-}=-iS^{R}\Sigma^{R}S_{0}^{<}+iS^{R}\Sigma^{<}S_{0}^{A}-iS^{<}\Sigma^{A}S_{0}^{A}.

(7.3)

Here, we define

\displaystyle\Sigma^{R}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Sigma^{++}-\Sigma^{+-}=\Sigma^{-+}-\Sigma^{--},

\displaystyle\Sigma^{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Sigma^{++}-\Sigma^{-+}=\Sigma^{+-}-\Sigma^{--},

\displaystyle i\Sigma^{+-}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Sigma^{<},

\displaystyle i\Sigma^{-+}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Sigma^{>},

(7.4)

and employ Eq. (
2.12
) for fermionic propagators
to derive the equations above. In this expression, the integral and
spinor indices are omitted, while the indices for the time branch
on the CTP are explicitly shown. Substituting Eq. (
7.3
)
into Eq. (
7.1
), we obtain

\displaystyle S^{<}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S_{0}^{<}-S_{0}^{R}\Sigma^{R}S^{<}+S_{0}^{R}\Sigma^{<}S^{A}-S_{0}^{<}\Sigma^{A}S^{A}

(7.5)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S_{0}^{<}-S^{R}\Sigma^{R}S_{0}^{<}+S^{R}\Sigma^{<}S_{0}^{A}-S^{<}\Sigma^{A}S_{0}^{A}.

The goal here is to rewrite Eq. (
7.5
) into the form
of the quantum kinetic equation.

The free parts of retarded and advanced propagators satisfy

\displaystyle-i\sigma\cdot D_{x_{1}}S_{0}^{R}(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta^{(4)}(x_{1}-x_{2}),

\displaystyle S_{0}^{A}(x_{1},x_{2})i\sigma\cdot\overleftarrow{D}_{x_{2}}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta^{(4)}(x_{1}-x_{2}).

(7.6)

Acting
i\sigma\cdot D_{x_{1}}
(
-i\sigma\cdot\overleftarrow{D}_{x_{2}}^{\dagger}
)
on the left (right) of both sides of the first (second) line of Eq. (
7.5
), respectively,
we obtain

\displaystyle i\sigma\cdot D_{x_{1}}S^{<}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Sigma^{R}S^{<}-\Sigma^{<}S^{A},

(7.7)

\displaystyle-S^{<}i\sigma\cdot\overleftarrow{D}_{x_{2}}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-S^{R}\Sigma^{<}+S^{<}\Sigma^{A},

(7.8)

where we employ Eqs. (
3.5
) and (
7.6
).
The retarded and advanced functions are further decomposed into

\displaystyle S^{R}=\overline{S}+\frac{i}{2}(S^{>}+S^{<}),\quad S^{A}=\overline{S}-\frac{i}{2}(S^{>}+S^{<}),

(7.9)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{R}=\overline{\Sigma}-\frac{i}{2}(\Sigma^{>}+\Sigma^{<}),\quad\Sigma^{A}=\overline{\Sigma}+\frac{i}{2}(\Sigma^{>}+\Sigma^{<}),

(7.10)

where
\overline{S}\equiv(S^{R}+S^{A})/2
and
\overline{\Sigma}\equiv(\Sigma^{R}+\Sigma^{A})/2
.
After performing the Wigner transformation,
\overline{S}
and
\overline{\Sigma}

will turn to the real part of the retarded propagator and self-energy
[
357
]
.
