Substituting these equations into Eqs. (
7.7
) and
(
7.8
), we obtain the quantum kinetic equation with
collisions in coordinate space,

\displaystyle(i\sigma\cdot D_{x_{1}}-\overline{\Sigma})S^{<}+\Sigma^{<}\overline{S}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{2}(\Sigma^{>}S^{<}-\Sigma^{<}S^{>}),

(7.11)

\displaystyle S^{<}(-i\sigma\cdot\overleftarrow{D}_{x_{2}}^{\dagger}-\overline{\Sigma})+\overline{S}\Sigma^{<}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2}(S^{<}\Sigma^{>}-S^{>}\Sigma^{<}).

(7.12)

Similarly, the greater propagators obey

\displaystyle(i\sigma\cdot D_{x_{1}}-\overline{\Sigma})S^{>}+\Sigma^{>}\overline{S}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{2}(\Sigma^{<}S^{>}-\Sigma^{>}S^{<}),

(7.13)

\displaystyle S^{>}(-i\sigma\cdot\overleftarrow{D}_{x_{2}}^{\dagger}-\overline{\Sigma})+\overline{S}\Sigma^{>}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2}(S^{>}\Sigma^{<}-S^{<}\Sigma^{>}).

(7.14)

As in Eq. (
3.5
) for free massless fermions,
these expressions are not gauge invariant. To obtain the gauge-invariant
ones, we consider the following gauge-invariant product,

\int d^{4}zU(x_{2},x_{1})A(x_{1},z)B(z,x_{2})=\int d^{4}zU_{C}(x_{1},x_{2},z)\widetilde{A}(x_{1},z)\widetilde{B}(z,x_{2}),

(7.15)

where
\widetilde{A}(x_{1},z)=A(x_{1},z)U(z,x_{1})
,
\widetilde{B}(z,x_{2})=B(z,x_{2})U(x_{2},z)
,
and we introduce

U_{C}(x_{2},x_{1},z)=\exp\left(-\frac{i}{\hbar}\int_{L}dx^{\mu}A_{\mu}\right)=U(x_{2},x_{1})U(x_{1},z)U(z,x_{2}).

(7.16)

Here
L
represents the closed path:
z\to x_{1}\to x_{2}\to z
, which has
a triangle shape. Equation (
7.15
)
is manifestly gauge invariant. As a compensation for the gauge invariance,

U_{C}(x_{1},x_{2},z)
is added to the product.

To change variables in the coordinate space to the phase space, let
us consider the Wigner transformation of the product of Eq. (
7.15
),

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}(x,p)\star\widetilde{B}(x,p)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\int d^{4}y\exp\left(\frac{i}{\hbar}p\cdot y\right)\int d^{4}zU_{C}\left(x-\frac{y}{2},x+\frac{y}{2},z+x\right)

(7.17)

\displaystyle\times\widetilde{A}\left(x+\frac{y}{2},z+x\right)\widetilde{B}\left(z+x,x-\frac{y}{2}\right),

where we have shifted the integral variable
z\to z+x
for convenience
of later use, and the operator
\star
is called the Moyal product,
whose explicit form is given above. For convenience, we express

\widetilde{A}
and
\widetilde{B}
as

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}\left(x+\frac{y}{2},z+x\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}u\cdot\partial_{x}\right)\widetilde{A}\left(x-\frac{v}{2},x+\frac{v}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}u\cdot\partial_{x}\right)\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\exp\left(\frac{i}{\hbar}p\cdot v\right)\widetilde{A}(x,p),

\displaystyle\widetilde{B}\left(z+x,x-\frac{y}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}v\cdot\partial_{x}\right)\widetilde{B}\left(x+\frac{u}{2},x-\frac{u}{2}\right)

(7.18)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}v\cdot\partial_{x}\right)\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\exp\left(-\frac{i}{\hbar}p\cdot u\right)\widetilde{B}(x,p),

where we introduced
u=z+y/2
,
v=z-y/2
.

Let us focus on
U_{C}
which, using the Stokes theorem, can be rewritten
as

U_{C}\left(x-\frac{y}{2},x+\frac{y}{2},z+x\right)=\exp\left[-\frac{i}{\hbar}\int_{L}d\xi^{\mu}A_{\mu}(\xi)\right]=\exp\left[-\frac{i}{2\hbar}\int_{S}d\xi^{\mu}\wedge d\xi^{\nu}F_{\mu\nu}(\xi)\right],

(7.19)

where
S
is the surface area bounded by
L
,
\xi^{\mu}
is the
parametrized coordinate to be defined below, and
\wedge
is the
exterior product such that
d\xi^{\mu}\wedge d\xi^{\nu}=-d\xi^{\nu}\wedge d\xi^{\mu}
.
The parametrized coordinate is defined as

\xi^{\mu}(s,t)=x^{\mu}+u^{\mu}s+v^{\mu}t,

(7.20)

where
s,t\in[-1/2,1/2]
and
s+t\geq 0
. Since
dt\wedge dt=ds\wedge ds=0

and
dt\wedge ds=-ds\wedge dt
, the surface element
d\xi^{\mu}\wedge d\xi^{\nu}

can be expressed as

d\xi^{\mu}\wedge d\xi^{\nu}=(u^{\mu}ds+v^{\mu}dt)\wedge(u^{\nu}ds+v^{\nu}dt)=2v^{\mu}u^{\nu}dt\wedge ds.

(7.21)

Therefore, we have

\int_{S}d\xi^{\mu}\wedge d\xi^{\nu}F_{\mu\nu}[\xi(s,t)]=2\int_{-1/2}^{1/2}dt\int_{-t}^{1/2}dsF_{\mu\nu}[\xi(s,t)]v^{\mu}u^{\nu}.

(7.22)

Expanding
F_{\mu\nu}[\xi(s,t)]
around
x
, we have

\displaystyle\int d\xi^{\mu}\wedge d\xi^{\nu}F_{\mu\nu}[\xi(s,t)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\int_{-1/2}^{1/2}dt\int_{-t}^{1/2}ds(su\cdot\partial+tv\cdot\partial)^{n}v^{\mu}u^{\nu}F_{\mu\nu}(x)

(7.23)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle H(u\cdot\partial_{x},v\cdot\partial_{x})v^{\mu}u^{\nu}F_{\mu\nu}(x).

Here we used the notation
F_{\mu\nu}[x]\equiv F_{\mu\nu}(x)
as
a function of space-time. With this formula, we can write
U_{C}

as

U_{C}\left(x-\frac{y}{2},x+\frac{y}{2},z+x\right)=\exp\left[-\frac{i}{2\hbar}H(u\cdot\partial_{x},v\cdot\partial_{x})v^{\mu}u^{\nu}F_{\mu\nu}(x)\right].

(7.24)

The integral in Eq. (
7.23
) can be analytically performed as

H(a,b)=\frac{2}{ab(a-b)}\left[b\exp\left(\frac{b-a}{2}\right)-a\exp\left(\frac{a-b}{2}\right)\right]+\frac{2}{ab}\exp\left(\frac{a+b}{2}\right).

(7.25)

Although
a
and
b
are on the denominator,
H(a,b)
is analytic
at the origin. For small
a
and
b
,
H(a,b)
is expanded as

H(a,b)=1+\frac{a+b}{6}+\frac{1}{24}(a^{2}+b^{2})+\frac{1}{240}(a+b)(a^{2}+b^{2})+\cdots.

(7.26)

In particular, if we set
b=0
, it reduces to a similar form to that
in Eq. (
3.16
),

H(a,0)=\frac{2}{a^{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{a}{2}\right)\left(e^{a}a-e^{a}+1\right)=j_{0}\left(i\frac{a}{2}\right)-ij_{1}\left(i\frac{a}{2}\right).

(7.27)

From Eqs. (
7.18
) and (
7.24
), the integrand in
Eq. (
7.17
) can be put into the form,

\displaystyle U_{C}\left(x-\frac{y}{2},x+\frac{y}{2},z+x\right)\widetilde{A}\left(x+\frac{y}{2},z+x\right)\widetilde{B}\left(z+x,x-\frac{y}{2}\right)

(7.28)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left[-\frac{i}{2\hbar}H(u\cdot\partial_{x},v\cdot\partial_{x})v^{\mu}u^{\nu}F_{\mu\nu}(x)\right]

\displaystyle\times\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}u\cdot\partial_{x^{\prime}}\right)\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}v\cdot\partial_{x^{\prime\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle\times\left.\widetilde{A}\left(x^{\prime}-\frac{v}{2},x^{\prime}+\frac{v}{2}\right)\widetilde{B}\left(x^{\prime\prime}+\frac{u}{2},x^{\prime\prime}-\frac{u}{2}\right)\right|_{x^{\prime}=x^{\prime\prime}=x}.

Note the spacetime derivative
\partial_{x}
in
H
only acts on

F_{\mu\nu}(x)
. The Wigner transformation replaces
u
and
v

with momentum derivatives. A straightforward calculation yields the
result of the Moyal product,

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}(x,p)\star\widetilde{B}(x,p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left[-\frac{i\hbar}{2}H(-i\hbar\partial_{p^{\prime\prime}}\cdot\partial_{x},i\hbar\partial_{p^{\prime}}\cdot\partial_{x})F_{\mu\nu}(x)\partial_{p^{\prime}}^{\mu}\partial_{p^{\prime\prime}}^{\nu}\right]

(7.29)

\displaystyle\times\exp\left[\frac{i\hbar}{2}\left(\partial_{x^{\prime\prime}}\cdot\partial_{p^{\prime}}-\partial_{x^{\prime}}\cdot\partial_{p^{\prime\prime}}\right)\right]\times\left.\widetilde{A}(x^{\prime},p^{\prime})\widetilde{B}(x^{\prime\prime},p^{\prime\prime})\right|_{x^{\prime}=x^{\prime\prime}=x,p^{\prime}=p^{\prime\prime}=x},

where
u
and
v
are replaced by
-i\hbar\partial_{p^{\prime\prime}}

and
i\hbar\partial_{p^{\prime}}
respectively. This product plays
an essential role in
\hbar
expansion. Up to
O(\hbar)
, the Moyal
product is expanded as

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}(x,p)\star\widetilde{B}(x,p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}(x,p)\widetilde{B}(x,p)+\frac{i\hbar}{2}\left\{\widetilde{A}(x,p),\widetilde{B}(x,p)\right\}_{{\rm P.B.}}

(7.30)

\displaystyle-\frac{i\hbar}{2}F_{\mu\nu}\partial_{p}^{\mu}\widetilde{A}(x,p)\partial_{p}^{\nu}\widetilde{B}(x,p)+O(\hbar^{2}),

where the Poisson bracket is defined as

\left\{\widetilde{A},\widetilde{B}\right\}_{{\rm P.B.}}\equiv(\partial_{p}^{\mu}\widetilde{A})(\partial_{\mu}^{x}\widetilde{B})-(\partial_{\mu}^{x}\widetilde{A})(\partial_{p}^{\mu}\widetilde{B}).

(7.31)

After performing the Wigner transformation of Eqs. (
7.11
)
and (
7.12
) multiplied by
U(x_{2},x_{1})
,
the Kadanoff-Baym equation is put into the form,

\displaystyle\sigma^{\mu}\left(\Pi_{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}i\hbar\nabla_{\mu}\right)S^{<}-\hbar\overline{\Sigma}\star S^{<}+\hbar\Sigma^{<}\star\overline{S}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i\hbar}{2}(\Sigma^{<}\star S^{>}-\Sigma^{>}\star S^{<}),

(7.32)

\displaystyle\left(\Pi_{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}i\hbar\nabla_{\mu}\right)S^{<}\sigma^{\mu}-\hbar S^{<}\star\overline{\Sigma}+\hbar\overline{S}\star\Sigma^{<}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{i\hbar}{2}(S^{>}\star\Sigma^{<}-S^{<}\star\Sigma^{>}).

(7.33)

Here we removed tildes from
\widetilde{S}
and
\widetilde{\Sigma}

for notational simplicity, and we employed the expression of Eqs. (
3.18
)
and (
3.19
) in first terms of Eqs. (
7.32
)
and (
7.33
), which are equivalent to
\sigma\cdot p\star S^{<}

and
S^{<}\star\sigma\cdot p
. To prove these expressions, we note
the covariant derivative
iD_{x_{1}\mu}
can be expressed as

i\check{D}_{\mu}(x_{1},z)=\left[-i\hbar\partial_{\mu}^{z}-A_{\mu}\left(\frac{x_{1}+z}{2}\right)\right]\delta^{4}(x_{1}-z),

(7.34)

in the product form (
7.2
) so that we have

iD_{\mu}^{x_{1}}S^{<}(x_{1},x_{2})=\int d^{4}zi\check{D}_{\mu}(x_{1},z)S^{<}(z,x_{2}).

(7.35)

The Wigner transformation of the gauge invariant covariant derivative

U(z,x_{1})i\check{D}_{\mu}(x_{1},z)
is evaluated as

\int d^{4}\overline{y}\exp\left(\frac{i}{\hbar}p\cdot\overline{y}\right)U\left(\overline{x}-\frac{\overline{y}}{2},\overline{x}+\frac{\overline{y}}{2}\right)\left[i\hbar\partial_{\mu}^{\overline{y}}-A_{\mu}(\overline{x})\right]\delta(\overline{y})=p_{\mu},

(7.36)

where
\overline{x}=(x_{1}+z)/2
,
\overline{y}=x_{1}-z
, and
\partial_{\mu}^{z}=-\partial_{\mu}^{\overline{y}}
. Therefore
the Wigner transformation of
\sigma^{\mu}iD_{\mu}^{x_{1}}S^{<}(x_{1},x_{2})

multiplied by
U(x_{2},x_{1})
turns to
\sigma\cdot p\star S^{<}
.
One can check
\sigma\cdot p\star S^{<}=\sigma^{\mu}\left[\Pi_{\mu}+(1/2)i\hbar\nabla_{\mu}\right]S^{<}

by using Eq. (
7.29
) together with Eq. (
7.27
).
We assign the self-energy at
O(\hbar)
such that it can reproduce
to the classical Boltzmann equation in the
\hbar\to 0
limit. Roughly
speaking, the right-hand sides in Eqs. (
7.32
)
and (
7.33
) represent collisions and
\overline{\Sigma}\star S^{<}

corresponds to the self-energy correction, while
\Sigma^{<}\star\overline{S}

has no counter part in the classical kinetic theory. Since
\overline{S}

represents the real part of the retarded propagator, it is negligible
in the quasi-particle approximation
[
357
]
.

Parametrizing
S=\overline{\sigma}^{\mu}S_{\mu}
,
\Sigma=\sigma^{\mu}\Sigma_{\mu}
,
and using the same technique discussed in Sec.
4
,
we find a set of equations,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}S^{<,\mu}+i\left[\overline{\Sigma}_{\mu},S^{<,\mu}\right]_{\star}-i\left[\Sigma_{\mu}^{<},\overline{S}^{\mu}\right]_{\star}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{\mu}^{\mu},

(7.37)

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mu}S^{<,\mu}-\frac{\hbar}{2}\left\{\overline{\Sigma}_{\mu},S^{<,\mu}\right\}_{\star}+\frac{\hbar}{2}\left\{\Sigma_{\mu}^{<},\overline{S}^{\mu}\right\}_{\star}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{4}D_{\mu}^{\mu},

(7.38)

\displaystyle\Pi^{[\nu}S^{<,\mu]}-\frac{\hbar}{2}\left\{\overline{\Sigma}^{[\nu},S^{<,\mu]}\right\}_{\star}+\frac{\hbar}{2}\left\{\Sigma^{<,[\nu},\overline{S}^{\mu]}\right\}_{\star}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\hbar}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\left(\nabla_{\rho}S_{\sigma}^{<}-C_{\rho\sigma}\right)+\frac{\hbar^{2}}{4}D^{[\mu\nu]},

where we introduced

\displaystyle C_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\left\{\Sigma_{\mu}^{<},S_{\nu}^{>}\right\}_{\star}-\left\{\Sigma_{\mu}^{>},S_{\nu}^{<}\right\}_{\star}\right),

(7.40)

\displaystyle D_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i\hbar}\left(\left[\Sigma_{\mu}^{<},S_{\nu}^{>}\right]_{\star}-\left[\Sigma_{\mu}^{>},S_{\nu}^{<}\right]_{\star}\right),

(7.41)

with
\{A,B\}_{\star}=A\star B+B\star A
,
[A,B]_{\star}=A\star B-B\star A
,
and
A^{[\mu}B^{\nu]}\equiv A^{\mu}B^{\nu}-A^{\nu}B^{\mu}
. From
Eqs. (
7.13
) and (
7.14
),
one can obtain the kinetic equations for
S^{>,\mu}
by exchanging

<
and
>
in Eqs. (
7.37
)-(
).
In particular, Eq. (
7.37
) represents the kinetic equation
that describes the time evolution of the system, while other equations
are constraints. In Eqs. (
7.37
)-(
),

\overline{\Sigma}_{\mu}
gives a correction to the dispersion relation,
like a thermal mass. If
\overline{\Sigma}_{\mu}
depends on
x
,
it plays a role of potential term in Eq. (
7.37
). As mentioned
above,
\overline{S}^{\mu}
is the real part of the retarded propagator,
which is negligible within the quasi-particle approximation
[
357
]
.
These contributions are common in an ordinary quantum kinetic theory. In the
following, we focus on collision effects, so that we neglect terms
that contain
\overline{\Sigma}_{\mu}
and
\overline{S}^{\mu}
.
Then, the chiral kinetic equation with collision terms (the transport
equation) has

\nabla_{\mu}S^{<,\mu}=C_{\mu}^{\mu}.

(7.42)

We can expand the propagator in powers of
\hbar
and solve it order
by order,

S^{<}=S_{(0)}^{<}+\hbar S_{(1)}^{<}+\hbar^{2}S_{(2)}^{<}+\cdots.

(7.43)

The leading order solution for
S_{(0)}^{<\mu}
is just
\mathscr{J}_{(0)}^{\rho}(x,p)

in Eq. (
4.15
) for right-handed fermions,

S_{(0)}^{<,\mu}=2\pi\mathrm{sgn}(p_{0})p^{\mu}f_{(0)}\delta(p^{2}),

(7.44)

where
\mathrm{sgn}(p_{0})=\Theta(p_{0})-\Theta(-p_{0})
is the sign function, and
we have pulled
2\pi\,\mathrm{sgn}(p_{0})
out of
f_{(0)}
in Eq. (
4.16
)
so that the new
f_{(0)}
will reduce to the Fermi-Dirac distribution
at thermal equilibrium,
f_{(0)}=f_{\rm FD}(p\cdot u-\mu_{+})
.
In the following, we focus on the positive energy state
p_{0}>0
, and
drop
\mathrm{sgn}(p_{0})
throughout this section unless specified otherwise.
The greater component is then

S_{(0)}^{>\mu}=2\pi p^{\mu}(1-f_{(0)})\delta(p^{2}).

(7.45)

At this order, the distribution satisfies the ordinary Boltzmann equation,

p^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}^{(0)}f_{(0)}=p^{\mu}C_{\mu},

(7.46)

with

C_{\mu}=\Sigma_{(0)\mu}^{<}(1-f_{(0)})-\Sigma_{(0)\mu}^{>}f_{(0)}.

(7.47)

The constraint equation (
) at
O(\hbar)

is modified by the self-energy,

p^{\nu}S_{(1)}^{<,\mu}-p^{\mu}S_{(1)}^{<,\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\left(\nabla_{\rho}^{(0)}S_{(0)\sigma}^{<}-\Sigma_{(0)\rho}^{<}S_{(0)\sigma}^{>}+\Sigma_{(0)\rho}^{>}S_{(0)\sigma}^{<}\right)=0.

(7.48)

The solution can be obtained
[
151
]
as

\displaystyle S_{(1)}^{<,\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi p^{\mu}f_{(1)}\delta(p^{2})+\pi\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}p_{\nu}F_{\rho\sigma}f_{(0)}\delta^{\prime}(p^{2})

(7.49)

\displaystyle+2\pi S_{(n)}^{\mu\nu}(\nabla_{\nu}^{(0)}f_{(0)}-C_{\nu})\delta(p^{2}),

where
S_{(n)}^{\mu\nu}
is defined by

S_{(n)}^{\mu\nu}\equiv\frac{\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}p_{\alpha}n_{\beta}}{2n\cdot p},

(7.50)

which is Eq. (
6.86
) with
\lambda=+1
.
We see that the side jump term is shifted in presence of the collision
term,

S_{(n)}^{\mu\nu}\nabla_{\nu}^{(0)}f_{(0)}\to S_{(n)}^{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{\nu}^{(0)}f_{(0)}-C_{\nu}\right)\delta(p^{2}).

(7.51)

The sum of Eqs. (
7.44
) and (
7.49
)
gives the solution up to
O(\hbar)
.

\displaystyle S^{<,\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi p^{\mu}f\delta(p^{2})+\pi\hbar\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}p_{\nu}F_{\rho\sigma}f\delta^{\prime}(p^{2})+2\pi\hbar S_{(n)}^{\mu\nu}(\nabla_{\nu}f-C_{\nu})\delta(p^{2})+O(\hbar^{2}),

(7.52)

where
f=f_{(0)}+\hbar f_{(1)}
. For
S^{>,\mu}
, one simply needs to replace
f
by
(1-f)
. When including both the contributions from fermions
(p\cdot n>0)
and anti-fermions
(p\cdot n<0)
, one has to multiply
S^{<,\mu}
or
S^{>,\mu}
with an overall factor,
{\rm sgn}(p\cdot n)
as in Eq. (
7.44
).
The existence of the collision kernel

C_{\nu}
in
S^{<,\mu}
is necessary to maintain the Lorentz covariance
of the current
[
149
]
, or equivalently, the frame independence
of
S^{<,\mu}
. One can show the frame independence by using
p^{\nu}(\nabla_{\nu}f-C_{\nu})=O(\hbar)
.
On the other hand, the CKT up to
O(\hbar)
with collisions for a generic frame vector
n^{\mu}(x)
is given by
[
151
,
168
]

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta\left(p^{2}-\hbar\frac{B_{(n)}\cdot p}{p\cdot n}\right)\Bigg[\bigg(p\cdot\hat{\mathcal{D}}+\frac{\hbar S^{\mu\nu}_{(n)}E_{(n)\mu}}{p\cdot n}\hat{\mathcal{D}}_{\nu}+\hbar S^{\mu\nu}_{(n)}\big(\partial_{\mu}F_{\rho\nu}\big)\partial^{\rho}_{p}+\hbar\big(\partial_{\mu}S^{\mu\nu}_{(n)}\big)\hat{\mathcal{D}}_{\nu}\bigg)f

(7.53)

\displaystyle-\hbar S^{\mu\nu}_{(n)}\Big((1-f)\nabla_{\mu}\Sigma^{<}_{\nu}-f\nabla_{\mu}\Sigma^{>}_{\nu}\Big)\Bigg],

where
\hat{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}f=\nabla_{\mu}f-{C}_{\mu}
and the nonlinear terms of self-energies are dropped
by assuming weak coupling.

E_{(n)\mu}
and
B_{(n)\mu}
are defined by replacing
u^{\mu}
by
n^{\mu}
in Eq. (
4.35
) as

E_{(n)\sigma}=F_{\sigma\rho}n^{\rho},\;B_{(n)\sigma}=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\sigma\mu\nu\rho}n^{\mu}F^{\nu\rho}.

(7.54)

So far, the collision terms are expressed in terms of self-energies
whose detailed forms depend on the theory. As an example for the self-energy
in the absence of the background field, let us consider the following
expression
[
151
]
,

\Sigma_{\mu}^{<}=\int_{p^{\prime},k,k^{\prime}}P(p,k,k^{\prime})S_{\mu}^{>}(p^{\prime})S^{<}(k)\cdot S^{<}(k^{\prime}),

(7.55)

where
P(p,k,k^{\prime})
and
\int_{p^{\prime},k,k^{\prime}}
are defined by

P(p,k,k^{\prime})\equiv 4e^{2}\left[\frac{1}{(p-k)^{2}}+\frac{1}{(p-k^{\prime})^{2}}\right]^{2},

(7.56)

and

\int_{p^{\prime},k,k^{\prime}}\equiv\int\frac{d^{4}p^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\int\frac{d^{4}k^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{4}}(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}(p+p^{\prime}-k-k^{\prime}).

(7.57)

This self-energy represents the Coulomb scattering of right-handed
fermions. Similarly, one can obtain
\Sigma_{\mu}^{>}
by interchanging

<
and
>
. The collision term in Eq. (
7.42
) becomes

\displaystyle C_{\mu}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{p^{\prime},k,k^{\prime}}P(p,k,k^{\prime})\left[S^{>}(p^{\prime})\cdot S^{>}(p)S^{<}(k)\cdot S^{<}(k^{\prime})\right.

(7.58)

\displaystyle\left.-S^{<}(p^{\prime})\cdot S^{<}(p)S^{>}(k)\cdot S^{>}(k^{\prime})\right].

The explicit expression of the collision term is a little bit complicated
because of the existence of
S_{(n)}^{\mu\nu}
. However, if we choose
the center of mass frame called the no-jump frame
[
149
]
,
the terms proportional to
S_{(n)}^{\mu\nu}
are canceled. Concretely,
we can choose

n_{c,\mu}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{s}}(p_{\mu}+p^{\prime}_{\mu})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{s}}(k_{\mu}+k^{\prime}_{\mu}),

(7.59)

with
s=(p+p^{\prime})^{2}
, then the lessor propagator becomes

\displaystyle S^{<,\mu}(x,p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi p^{\mu}f^{(n_{c})}(x,p)\delta(p^{2})

(7.60)

\displaystyle+\pi\hbar\frac{\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}p_{\alpha}p^{\prime}_{\beta}}{p\cdot p^{\prime}}\left[\nabla_{\nu}f^{(n_{c})}(x,p)-C_{\nu}\right]\delta(p^{2}).

Therefore, the term of
O(\hbar)
vanishes in
S^{<}(x,p)\cdot S^{<}(x,p^{\prime})

and we obtain

\displaystyle S^{<}(x,p)\cdot S^{<}(x,p^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(2\pi)^{2}(p\cdot p^{\prime})f^{(n_{c})}(x,p^{\prime})f^{(n_{c})}(x,p)

(7.61)

\displaystyle\times\delta(p^{2})\delta(p^{\prime 2})+O(\hbar^{2}).

In the no-jump frame, the collision term
C_{\mu}^{\mu}=2\pi p^{\mu}C_{\mu}\delta(p^{2})

reduces to the form of standard one with
p^{\mu}C_{\mu}
given
by

\displaystyle p^{\mu}C_{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}}|\mathcal{M}|^{2}\left\{\left[1-f^{(n_{c})}(x,p)\right]\left[1-f^{(n_{c})}(x,p^{\prime})\right]f^{(n_{c})}(k)f^{(n_{c})}(x,k^{\prime})\right.

(7.62)

\displaystyle\left.-f^{(n_{c})}(x,p)f^{(n_{c})}(x,p^{\prime})\left[1-f^{(n_{c})}(x,k)\right]\left[1-f^{(n_{c})}(x,k^{\prime})\right]\right\},

where
|\mathcal{M}|^{2}=4P(p^{\prime},k,k^{\prime})(k\cdot k^{\prime})(p\cdot p^{\prime})
, all
momenta are now on-shell with positive energy
1
1

1

\color
blue
Here, we just focus on the Coulomb scattering process. In general, the self-energy (
7.55
) also contains
the fermion and anti-fermion annihilation process, when one takes into account the anti-fermion contribution.
, e.g.,
k^{\mu}=(|\mathbf{k}|,\mathbf{k})
,
and the integral symbol
\int_{\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}}

is defined by

\int_{\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}}\equiv\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{2|\mathbf{p}^{\prime}|}\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{2|\mathbf{k}|}\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{2|\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|}(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}(p+p^{\prime}-k-k^{\prime}).

(7.63)

One can check that the collision term vanishes if the global equilibrium
solution obtained in Sec.
4.2
is employed.

Moreover, when choosing
n^{\mu}(x)=u^{\mu}(x)
, a local-equilibrium solution for Wigner functions satisfying the vanishing collision term of the Coulomb scattering is found
[
168
]
,

\displaystyle S_{\text{leq}}^{<,\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi\,{\rm sgn}(p\cdot u)\Bigg[\delta(p^{2})\left(p^{\mu}+\frac{\hbar}{2}\big(u^{\mu}(p\cdot\omega)-\omega^{\mu}(p\cdot u)\big)\partial_{p\cdot u}-\hbar S^{\mu\nu}_{(u)}\tilde{E}_{\nu}\partial_{p\cdot u}\right)

(7.64)

\displaystyle+\frac{\hbar\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}F_{\alpha\beta}}{4}\partial_{p\nu}\delta(p^{2})\Bigg]f_{\rm FD}(p\cdot u-\mu_{+}),

where

\displaystyle\tilde{E}_{\nu}=E_{\nu}+T\partial_{\nu}(\beta\mu_{+})+\frac{(p\cdot u)}{T}\partial_{\nu}T-p^{\sigma}(\zeta_{\nu\sigma}+\kappa_{\nu\sigma})

(7.65)

with
\zeta_{\mu\nu}=(\partial_{\mu}u_{\nu}+\partial_{\nu}u_{\mu})/2
and
\kappa_{\mu\nu}=(u_{\mu}u\cdot\partial u_{\nu}-u_{\nu}u\cdot\partial u_{\mu})/2
. Here we also incorporate the contribution from anti-fermions. Even though the
\hbar
corrections in Eq. (
7.64
) only yield a non-vanishing current proportional to
\omega^{\mu}
as the CVE after integrating over
p
, other terms could still affect the spectrum of spin polarization in momentum space. Nonetheless, the local-equilibrium solution does not satisfy the free-streaming part of the kinetic equation, and non-equilibrium corrections pertinent to interaction have to be included. Such contributions further engender nonlinear chiral transport (e.g., viscous corrections on CME and CVE) investigated in Refs.
[
76
,
77
,
167
,
170
,
168
,
173
]
by adopting the relaxation-time approximation.

We have derived in this section the Kadanoff-Baym equation with collision
terms for massless fermions. It is straightforward to extend the results
for massless fermions to the case of massive fermions, which will
be discussed in Sec.
8
and
9
.

8  Quantum kinetic theory for massive fermions without collisions

To generalize the chiral
kinetic theory (CKT) for massless fermions to the quantum kinetic
theory (QKT) for massive fermions, we should identify several distinct
features between the massless and massive cases. Since chiral symmetry
is explicitly broken by the fermionic mass, it is no longer suitable
to decompose Wigner functions into chiral components. Furthermore,
the right-handed and left-handed components are now entangled even
in the absence of collisions. On the other hand, the orientation of spin
of massive particles is also no longer locked by the momentum direction
as for massless particles. One hence has to treat the spin degrees
of freedom as independent variables. In fact, the chirality mixing
has implied the intertwined dynamics of charge and spin transport
for massive fermions without collisions. Such a QKT, in the collisionless
case, has been recently derived from Wigner functions in Refs.
[
335
,
336
,
338
,
346
]

with follow-up studies in, e.g., Refs.
[
340
,
388
,
389
,
344
,
390
,
389
,
161
,
391
,
392
,
393
]
.
In this section, we will mostly follow the approach in Ref.
[
338
]
,
which makes a smooth connection to the CKT in the massless limit and
covers the similar formalism constructed in Refs.
[
335
,
336
]
.
The inclusion of collisions into the QKT for massive fermions will
be done in the next section.

8.1  Master equations and Wigner functions

To work with suitable bases, it is more convenient to decompose the
(gauge-invariant) Wigner function for Dirac fermions in generators
of the Clifford algebra
[
362
]
,

\widetilde{W}=\mathcal{F}+i\mathcal{P}\gamma^{5}+\mathcal{V}^{\mu}\gamma_{\mu}+\mathcal{A}^{\mu}\gamma^{5}\gamma_{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{S}^{\mu\nu}\sigma_{\mu\nu},

(8.1)

where
\sigma_{\mu\nu}=i[\gamma_{\mu},\gamma_{\nu}]/2
and
\gamma^{5}=i\gamma^{0}\gamma^{1}\gamma^{2}\gamma^{3}
.
For notational brevity, hereafter we will remove the tilde of
\widetilde{W}

and write
W=\widetilde{W}
as the gauge invariant Wigner function.
In this section, we will take
W=W^{<}
and drop the superscript

<
for Clifford-algebra coefficients unless specified otherwise.
The coefficients
\mathcal{V}^{\mu}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\mu}
contribute
to the vector and axial charge currents, while
\mathcal{F}
and

\mathcal{P}
are related to quark (scalar) and chiral (pseudo-scalar)
condensates, respectively. The antisymmetric
\mathcal{S}^{\mu\nu}

is related to magnetization. Note that all these coefficients are
real functions in phase space since
W^{\dagger}=\gamma^{0}W\gamma^{0}
.
Nevertheless, these coefficients are not independent of each other.
Their relations are governed by the kinetic equation without collisions
and the Kadanoff-Baym equation with collisions. Eventually, the independent
components should encode four dynamical variables that lead to a
2\times 2

matrix in spin space constructed by two non-local fermionic fields
in the Wigner function. This fact will be manifested by the derivation
of the Wigner function explicitly from a mode expansion of Dirac fields
in the next subsection.

In light of the derivation for massless fermions in Sec.
7
,
the Kadanoff-Baym equation for massive fermions can be written as

(\gamma_{\mu}\Pi^{\mu}-m)W^{<}+\frac{1}{2}i\hbar\gamma_{\mu}\nabla^{\mu}W^{<}=\frac{1}{2}i\hbar\Big(\Sigma^{<}\star W^{>}-\Sigma^{>}\star W^{<}\Big),

(8.2)

where
\Pi^{\mu}=p^{\mu}+O(\hbar^{2})
with
\nabla^{\mu}=\partial_{x}^{\mu}-F^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}^{p}+O(\hbar^{2})
,

m
is the mass of the fermion,
W^{\lessgtr}(x,p)
denote the Wigner
transformation of
G^{\lessgtr}(x_{1},x_{2})
in Eq. (
2.12
),
and
\Sigma^{\lessgtr}
demote the self-energies for fermions as
functions of
x
and
p
. In the derivation of (
8.2
),
we retained necessary terms for obtaining the first-order quantum
corrections in
\hbar
expansion and dropped all higher-order terms.
We then consider the collisionless case,
\Sigma^{\lessgtr}=0
, and
thus focus on just
W=W^{<}
. Inserting Eq. (
8.1
)
into Eq. (
8.2
) without collisions and then separating
real and imaginary parts in terms of independent spinor bases yield
10 equations with 32 degrees of freedom
[
362
,
338
,
336
]
.
Three of them read

\displaystyle m\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Pi\cdot\mathcal{V},

\displaystyle m\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\hbar}{2}\nabla_{\mu}\mathcal{A}^{\mu},

\displaystyle m\mathcal{S}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\Pi^{\rho}\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}+\frac{\hbar}{2}\nabla_{[\mu}\mathcal{V}_{\nu]},

(8.3)

where
A_{[\mu}B_{\nu]}\equiv A_{\mu}B_{\nu}-B_{\nu}A_{\mu}
. Accordingly,
one can choose either
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{P}
, and
\mathcal{S}^{\mu\nu}

as a set of independent variables, or the other components
\mathcal{V}^{\mu}

and
\mathcal{A}^{\mu}

[
394
]
, or
\mathcal{F}
and

\mathcal{S}^{\mu\nu}

[
336
]
, or
\mathcal{F}

and
\mathcal{A}^{\mu}

[
335
]
, as independent variables.
All these choices are physically equivalent. In this article, we choose

\mathcal{V}^{\mu}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\mu}
as an example and apply
the
\hbar
expansion to the rest of equations, which results in

6
master equations
[
338
]
,

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\mathcal{V}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(8.4)

\displaystyle(p^{2}-m^{2})\mathcal{V}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\hbar\widetilde{F}_{\mu\nu}\mathcal{A}^{\nu},

(8.5)

\displaystyle p_{\nu}\mathcal{V}_{\mu}-p_{\mu}\mathcal{V}_{\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\hbar}{2}\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\nabla^{\rho}\mathcal{A}^{\sigma},

(8.6)

\displaystyle p\cdot\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(8.7)

\displaystyle(p^{2}-m^{2})\mathcal{A}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\hbar}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}p_{\sigma}\nabla_{\nu}\mathcal{V}_{\rho},

(8.8)

\displaystyle p\cdot\nabla\mathcal{A}^{\mu}+F^{\nu\mu}\mathcal{A}_{\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\hbar}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}(\partial_{\sigma}F_{\beta\nu})\partial_{p}^{\beta}\mathcal{V}_{\rho},

(8.9)

where one redundant equation that can be obtained from the above set
has been omitted. Notably, integrating over
p
for Eq. (
8.6
)
corresponds to the conservation of angular momentum, where the right-hand
side lead to a non-vanishing anti-symmetric component of the canonical
energy-momentum tensor responsible for the angular-momentum transfer
from the orbital part to the spin part. A detailed analysis for the
massless case can be found in Ref.
[
175
]
. Consequently,
solving master equations (
8.4
)-(
8.9
) has implicitly
incorporated the quantum correction associated with the spin-orbit
interaction.

In the massless case, the two sets of master equations, Eqs. (
8.4
)-(
8.6
)
and Eqs. (
8.7
)-(
8.9
), become degenerate due to the
decoupling of
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{P}
, and
\mathcal{S}^{\mu\nu}

as manifested by Eq. (
8.3
); see also discussions
in Sec.
4
. Now, Eqs. (
8.4
)-(
8.6
)
are similar to those in the massless case whereby one acquires the
on-shell condition from Eq. (
8.5
) and perturbatively solves
for the extra
\hbar
correction on
\mathcal{V}^{\mu}
from Eq. (
8.6
)
and finally derives the kinetic equation from Eq. (
8.4
).
However, as opposed to Eqs. (
8.4
)-(
8.6
), the extra

\hbar
correction for
\mathcal{A}^{\mu}
cannot be fully solved
from Eq. (
8.7
), while Eq. (
8.8
) and Eq. (
8.9
)
still dictates the on-shell condition and yields the kinetic equation,
respectively. To determine the extra
\hbar
correction on
\mathcal{A}^{\mu}

with a smooth connection to the massless limit, we shall resort to
an alternative approach as presented in the next subsection. As mentioned
previously,
\mathcal{V}^{\mu}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\mu}
should contain
four dynamical variables.

We now seek perturbative solutions
\mathcal{V}^{\mu}=\mathcal{V}_{0}^{\mu}+\hbar\mathcal{V}_{1}^{\mu}

and
\mathcal{A}^{\mu}=\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\mu}+\hbar\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\mu}

up to
O(\hbar)
. The zeroth-order solutions are immediately obtained
from Eqs. (
8.5
)-(
8.8
) as

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{0}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi p^{\mu}\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})f_{V}(x,p),

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi a^{\mu}\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})f_{A}(x,p),

(8.10)

where
f_{V}(x,p)
and
f_{A}(x,p)
represent the vector and axial
distribution functions respectively, and
a^{\mu}(x,p)
satisfies

p\cdot a=p^{2}-m^{2}
and corresponds to a non-normalized spin four-vector. Similar to the convention in Sec.
7
,
f_{V}(x,p)
and
f_{A}(x,p)
only incorporate the contributions from fermions with positive energy, while those from anti-fermions are neglected in the present and next sections unless specified otherwise.
In the massless limit, we have
a^{\mu}=p^{\mu}
because the spin
is enslaved by the momentum. However,
a^{\mu}
is a dynamical variable
in the massive case, which should be determined by the kinetic theory.
Usually, one may simply introduce

\widetilde{a}^{\mu}\equiv a^{\mu}f_{A},

(8.11)

without the decomposition adapted for making a comparison with the
massless result. Due to the constraint from Eq. (
8.8
),
f_{V}

and
\widetilde{a}^{\mu}
in total delineate four dynamical variables
as expected.

Now, the solution of
\mathcal{V}^{\mu}
from Eqs. (
8.4
)-(
8.6
)
up to
O(\hbar)
reads
[
338
]

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi\left\{\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})\left[p^{\mu}f_{V}+\frac{\hbar\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}n_{\nu}}{2p\cdot n}(\nabla_{\rho}\widetilde{a}_{\sigma}+F_{\rho\sigma}f_{A})\right]\right.

(8.12)

\displaystyle\left.+\hbar\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}\widetilde{a}_{\nu}\delta^{\prime}(p^{2}-m^{2})\right\}.

Here
n^{\mu}
corresponds to a timelike frame vector analogous to
the massless case. The solution of
\mathcal{A}^{\mu}
from Eqs. (
8.7
)-(
8.9
)
up to
O(\hbar)
is given by
[
338
]

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi\left[\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})\left(\widetilde{a}^{\mu}+\hbar S_{m(n)}^{\mu\nu}\nabla_{\nu}f_{V}\right)+\hbar\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}p_{\nu}\delta^{\prime}(p^{2}-m^{2})f_{V}\right],

(8.13)

where the tensor
S_{m(n)}^{\mu\nu}
is defined as

S_{m(n)}^{\mu\nu}=\frac{\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}p_{\alpha}n_{\beta}}{2a\cdot n}=\frac{\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}p_{\alpha}n_{\beta}}{2(p\cdot n+m)}.

(8.14)

For completeness, we should, in principle, multiply
\mathcal{V}^{\mu}

and
\mathcal{A}^{\mu}
by the sign function for energy to include
antiparticle contributions that have been given explicitly in Refs.

[
336
,
335
]
. As already mentioned, for notational simplicity,
we will mostly focus on just the positive-energy solutions. Here only
the
\hbar
correction proportional to
\delta^{\prime}(p^{2}-m^{2})

in Eq. (
8.13
) is solved from Eq. (
8.8
), whereas
the
S_{m(n)}^{\mu\nu}
term cannot be determined by Eq. (
8.7
),
which will be alternatively derived from a different approach. The

\hbar
terms containing the Levi-Civita symbols in Eqs. (
8.12
) and (
8.13
)
are dubbed as the magnetization-current terms associated with the
spin-orbit interaction, which correspond to the side-jump terms in
the massless limit. When taking
a^{\mu}=p^{\mu}
and
m=0
, one
can show that Eqs. (
8.12
) and (
8.13
) reduce to
the Wigner function of massless fermions presented in Sec.
4
.
Notably, unlike massless fermions, one can choose
n^{\mu}=n_{r}^{\mu}(p)=p^{\mu}/m

as the rest frame of a massive fermion. One finds
S_{m(n_{r})}^{\mu\nu}=0
;
hence Eqs. (
8.12
) and (
8.13
) and their corresponding
kinetic equations reduce to simpler forms. Such a frame choice has
been implicitly implemented in, e.g., Refs.
[
336
,
335
]
.
However, the price of such a frame choice is that a smooth connection
to the massless limit or more precisely to the small-mass regime for

mW\ll|\gamma\cdot\Delta W|
is lost.

Because of the explicit frame dependence of magnetization-current
terms, we anticipate also the frame dependence of
f_{V}
and
\widetilde{a}^{\mu}

as in the massless case. Based on the frame independence of
\mathcal{V}^{\mu}

and
\mathcal{A}^{\mu}
,
f_{V}
and
\widetilde{a}^{\mu}
follow
the modified frame transformations between arbitrary frames
of
n^{\mu}
and
n^{\prime\mu}

[
338
]
,

f_{V}^{(n^{\prime})}-f_{V}^{(n)}=\frac{\hbar\epsilon^{\lambda\nu\rho\sigma}n_{\lambda}n_{\nu}^{\prime}}{2(p\cdot n)(p\cdot n^{\prime})}\big(\nabla_{\rho}a_{\sigma}+F_{\rho\sigma}\big)f_{A},

(8.15)

and

\widetilde{a}^{(n^{\prime})\mu}-\widetilde{a}^{(n)\mu}=\frac{\hbar\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}}{2}\left(\frac{n_{\beta}}{p\cdot n+m}-\frac{n_{\beta}^{\prime}}{p\cdot n^{\prime}+m}\right)p_{\alpha}\nabla_{\nu}f_{V}.

(8.16)

8.2  Magnetization-current terms from Dirac wave-functions

In this subsection, we employ an alternative method to derive Wigner
functions without background fields up to
O(\hbar)
presented in
Ref.
[
338
]
(see also Ref.
[
342
]
).
In particular, we will utilize the result to determine the magnetization-current
term in
\mathcal{A}_{\mu}
. Additionally, the origin of
f_{V}

and
\widetilde{a}^{\mu}
in quantum field theory will be manifested. We
now start with a free Dirac field
[
370
]
,

\displaystyle\psi(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{3}{\bf k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2E_{k}}}\sum_{s}u_{s}(p)e^{-ik\cdot x}a_{s,{\bf k}},

(8.17)

where
k_{0}=k^{0}=E_{k}=\sqrt{|{\bf k}|^{2}+m^{2}}
and we have
dropped the anti-fermion part for simplicity. We have the annihilation
(creation) operators
a_{s,{\bf k}}
(
a_{s,{\bf k}}^{\dagger}
)
and the wave function
u_{s}(k)=(\sqrt{k\cdot\sigma}\xi_{s},\sqrt{k\cdot\overline{\sigma}}\xi_{s})^{T}

with
\xi_{s}
being a two-component spinor with a spin index
s=\pm 1/2

[
370
]
.
In
u_{s}(k)
, we introduced
\sigma^{\mu}=e_{a}^{\mu}\sigma^{a}

from the local transformation of
\sigma^{a}=(1,\bm{\sigma})

via the vierbein
e_{a}^{\mu}
and similarly
\overline{\sigma}^{\mu}=e_{a}^{\mu}\overline{\sigma}^{a}

from
\overline{\sigma}^{a}=(1,-\bm{\sigma})
with
\bm{\sigma}=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3})
.
The frame vector mentioned in Sec.
5.3
can be
accordingly defined as
n^{\mu}\equiv e_{0}^{\mu}

[
173
]
.
The Wigner function then takes the form,

\displaystyle W(x,p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{k}_{-}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{k}_{+}}{(2\pi)^{3}}e^{-ik_{-}\cdot x}\frac{1}{\sqrt{4E_{k_{+}+k_{-}/2}E_{k_{+}-k_{-}/2}}}(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}\left(p-k_{+}\right)

(8.18)

\displaystyle\times\sum_{s,s^{\prime}}u_{s}\left(k_{+}+k_{-}/2\right)\overline{u}_{s^{\prime}}\left(k_{+}-k_{-}/2\right)\left\langle a_{s^{\prime},\mathbf{k}_{+}-\mathbf{k}_{-}/2}^{\dagger}a_{s,\mathbf{k}_{+}+\mathbf{k}_{-}/2}\right\rangle,

where
k_{+}^{\mu}=(k+k^{\prime})^{\mu}/2
and
k_{-}^{\mu}=(k-k^{\prime})^{\mu}

with
k
and
k^{\prime}
being on-shell momenta. One can integrate
over
\mathbf{k}_{+}
to obtain

\displaystyle W(x,p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{k}_{-}}{(2\pi)^{3}}e^{-ik_{-}\cdot x}\frac{1}{\sqrt{E_{p+k_{-}/2}E_{p-k_{-}/2}}}

(8.19)

\displaystyle\times\delta\left(p_{0}-\frac{1}{2}E_{p+k_{-}/2}-\frac{1}{2}E_{p-k_{-}/2}\right)

\displaystyle\times\sum_{s,s^{\prime}}u_{s}\left(p+\frac{1}{2}k_{-}\right)\overline{u}_{s^{\prime}}\left(p-\frac{1}{2}k_{-}\right)\left\langle a_{s^{\prime},\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{k}_{-}/2}^{\dagger}a_{s,\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k}_{-}/2}\right\rangle.

On the other hand, the expectation value of the density operator can
be written as

\langle a_{s^{\prime},{\bf k^{\prime}}}^{\dagger}a_{s,{\bf k}}\rangle=\delta_{ss^{\prime}}N_{V}({\bf k},{\bf k^{\prime}})+\mathcal{A}_{ss^{\prime}}({\bf k},{\bf k^{\prime}}).

(8.20)

Next, we parametrize the following spin sums as

\displaystyle\sum_{s}\xi_{s}\xi_{s}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n\cdot\sigma=I,

\displaystyle\sum_{s,s^{\prime}}\xi_{s}\mathcal{A}_{ss^{\prime}}\xi_{s^{\prime}}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S\cdot\sigma,

(8.21)

where the four vectors
n^{\mu}
and
S^{\mu}
satisfy
S\cdot n=0
.
Here the frame vector
n^{\mu}
also naturally appear as the choice
of a spin basis for the decomposition in
\sigma^{\mu}
. For simplicity,
we will work in the frame
n^{\mu}=(1,\bm{0})
for the follow-up
computations in this subsection. After the Wigner transformation, we define

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{V}(x,p)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{3}\bm{k}_{-}}{(2\pi)^{3}}N_{V}\left(\mathbf{p}+\frac{\mathbf{k}_{-}}{2},\mathbf{p}-\frac{\mathbf{k}_{-}}{2}\right)e^{-ik_{-}\cdot x},

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}^{\mu}(x,p)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{3}\bm{k}_{-}}{(2\pi)^{3}}S^{\mu}\left(\mathbf{p}+\frac{\mathbf{k}_{-}}{2},\mathbf{p}-\frac{\mathbf{k}_{-}}{2}\right)e^{-ik_{-}\cdot x},

(8.22)

where
\widetilde{f}_{V}(x,p)
and
\widetilde{S}^{\mu}(x,p)
are
related to the vector and spin-vector distribution functions, respectively.
From the construction above, it is clear to see
\widetilde{f}_{V}(x,p)

and
\widetilde{S}_{\mu}(x,p)
carry four degrees of freedom. We
will then reparameterize
\widetilde{f}_{V}(x,p)
and
\widetilde{S}^{\mu}(x,p)

in terms of
f_{V}(x,p)
and
\widetilde{a}^{\mu}(x,p)
.

According to Eq. (
8.21
), one has

\displaystyle\sum_{s}u_{s}\left(p+\frac{k_{-}}{2}\right)\overline{u}_{s}\left(p-\frac{k_{-}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\sqrt{\sigma\cdot\left(p+\frac{k_{-}}{2}\right)\overline{\sigma}\cdot\left(p-\frac{k_{-}}{2}\right)}&\sqrt{\sigma\cdot\left(p+\frac{k_{-}}{2}\right)\sigma\cdot\left(p-\frac{k_{-}}{2}\right)}\\
\sqrt{\overline{\sigma}\cdot\left(p+\frac{k_{-}}{2}\right)\overline{\sigma}\cdot\left(p-\frac{k_{-}}{2}\right)}&\sqrt{\overline{\sigma}\cdot\left(p+\frac{k_{-}}{2}\right)\sigma\cdot\left(p-\frac{k_{-}}{2}\right)}\end{array}\right),

and

\displaystyle\sum_{s,s^{\prime}}u_{s}\left(p+\frac{k_{-}}{2}\right)\mathcal{A}_{ss^{\prime}}\overline{u}_{s^{\prime}}\left(p-\frac{k_{-}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\sqrt{\sigma\cdot\left(p+\frac{k_{-}}{2}\right)}\sigma\cdot S\sqrt{\overline{\sigma}\cdot\left(p-\frac{k_{-}}{2}\right)}&\sqrt{\sigma\cdot\left(p+\frac{k_{-}}{2}\right)}\sigma\cdot S\sqrt{\sigma\cdot\left(p-\frac{k_{-}}{2}\right)}\\
\sqrt{\overline{\sigma}\cdot\left(p+\frac{k_{-}}{2}\right)}\sigma\cdot S\sqrt{\overline{\sigma}\cdot\left(p-\frac{k_{-}}{2}\right)}&\sqrt{\overline{\sigma}\cdot\left(p+\frac{k_{-}}{2}\right)}\sigma\cdot S\sqrt{\sigma\cdot\left(p-\frac{k_{-}}{2}\right)}\end{array}\right).

Note that the upper and lower off-diagonal terms in Eqs. (
8.2
) and (
)
are associated with
\mathcal{V}_{\mu}-\mathcal{A}_{\mu}
and
\mathcal{V}_{\mu}+\mathcal{A}_{\mu}
,
respectively. The diagonal terms are in connection with other components

\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{P}
, and
\mathcal{S}^{\mu\nu}
in Wigner
functions. To evaluate the matrix elements above, we will employ the
following tricks for Pauli matrices. We may write

\displaystyle p\cdot\sigma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m\exp\left(\widetilde{p}_{\perp}\cdot\sigma\theta\right),

\displaystyle\theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tanh^{-1}\left(\frac{|{\bf p}_{\perp}|}{E_{p}}\right),

(8.30)

where

p_{\perp}^{\mu}\equiv p^{\mu}-(n\cdot p)n^{\mu}\;,\;\;\;\widetilde{p}_{\perp}^{\mu}=p_{\perp}^{\mu}/\sqrt{-p_{\perp}^{2}}=p_{\perp}^{\mu}/|\bm{p}_{\perp}|\;,

(8.31)

yielding

\displaystyle\sqrt{p\cdot\sigma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{m}\left(\cosh\frac{\theta}{2}+\widetilde{p}_{\perp}\cdot\sigma\sinh\frac{\theta}{2}\right)

(8.32)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{1}{2(E_{p}+m)}}\left(E_{p}+m+p_{\perp}\cdot\sigma\right).

Hereafter we will use the subscripts
\perp
to denote the component
of an arbitrary vector perpendicular to the frame vector
n^{\mu}
.

Subsequently, we further make the
\hbar
expansion implicitly led
by the
k_{-}^{\mu}
expansion for wave functions by analogy with
the derivation in Ref.
[
151
]
for Weyl fermions.
More precisely, the leading-order terms with
k_{-}^{\mu}=0
in the
integrand of Eq. (
8.18
) contribute to the classical
contributions to Wigner functions, whereas
O(\hbar)
corrections
arise from the terms linear to
k_{-}^{\mu}
. After a tedious yet
straightforward computation for Eq. (
8.18
) by using
above relations (see Ref.
[
338
]
for details), up
to
O(k_{-})
, it is found

\displaystyle\sigma\cdot(\mathcal{V}-\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi\delta\left(p_{0}-E_{p}\right)\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{k}_{-}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{e^{-ik_{-}\cdot x}}{2E_{p}(E_{p}+m)}\left(\mathscr{N}^{+}\cdot\sigma N_{V}+\mathscr{S}^{+}\cdot\sigma\right),

\displaystyle\overline{\sigma}\cdot(\mathcal{V}+\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi\delta\left(p_{0}-E_{p}\right)\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{k}_{-}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{e^{-ik_{-}\cdot x}}{2E_{p}(E_{p}+m)}\left(\mathscr{N}^{-}\cdot\sigma N_{V}+\mathscr{S}^{-}\cdot\sigma\right),

(8.33)

where
\mathscr{N}_{\mu}^{+}
and
\mathscr{S}_{\mu}^{+}
are given
by

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}^{+}\cdot n

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2E_{p}\left(E_{p}+m\right),

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}_{\perp\mu}^{+}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\left(E_{p}+m\right)p_{\perp\mu}-i\epsilon_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}n^{\alpha}p^{\beta}k_{-}^{\nu},

\displaystyle\mathscr{S}^{+}\cdot n

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\left(E_{p}+m\right)p_{\perp}\cdot S_{\perp}+i\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}p_{\mu}k_{-\nu}n_{\alpha}S_{\beta},

\displaystyle\mathscr{S}_{\perp\mu}^{+}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\left[m\left(E_{p}+m\right)S_{\perp\mu}-\left(S_{\perp}\cdot p_{\perp}\right)p_{\perp\mu}\right]

(8.34)

\displaystyle-i\epsilon_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}n^{\alpha}S^{\beta}\left[\frac{p_{\perp}\cdot k_{-}}{E_{p}}p^{\nu}+\left(E_{p}+m\right)k_{-}^{\nu}\right],

Thus, one obtains

\displaystyle n\cdot\mathcal{V}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})E_{p}f_{V},

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{\perp\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})\left[p_{\perp\mu}f_{V}-\frac{1}{2E_{p}}\epsilon_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}n^{\alpha}\partial^{\nu}\left(\frac{p\cdot\widetilde{S}^{\beta}}{E_{p}+m}-m\widetilde{S}^{\beta}\right)\right],

\displaystyle n\cdot\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})p_{\perp}\cdot\widetilde{S}_{\perp},

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\perp\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})\left[\frac{\widetilde{S}_{\perp}\cdot p_{\perp}}{E_{p}+m}p_{\perp\mu}-m\widetilde{S}_{\perp\mu}-\frac{1}{2(E_{p}+m)}\epsilon_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}n^{\alpha}p^{\beta}\partial^{\nu}\widetilde{f}_{V}\right],

(8.35)

where we have re-parameterized

f_{V}(p,X)=\widetilde{f}_{V}(p,X)+\frac{1}{2E_{p}(E_{p}+m)}\epsilon^{\rho\nu\alpha\beta}n_{\alpha}p_{\beta}\partial_{\nu}\widetilde{S}_{\rho}(p,X).

(8.36)

Finally, by further taking

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}\cdot p_{\perp}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a\cdot nf_{A},

\displaystyle\frac{p\cdot\widetilde{S}}{E_{p}+m}p_{\perp\mu}-m\widetilde{S}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{\perp\mu}f_{A},

(8.37)

and retrieving the
\hbar
parameters, we arrive at

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})\left[p_{\mu}f_{V}+\hbar\frac{\epsilon_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}n^{\beta}}{2(p\cdot n)}\partial^{\nu}(a^{\alpha}f_{A})\right],

(8.38)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})\left[a_{\mu}f_{A}+\hbar\frac{\epsilon_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}p^{\alpha}n^{\beta}}{2(p\cdot n+m)}\partial^{\nu}f_{V}\right],

(8.39)

where we have replaced
E_{p}
by
p\cdot n
. We see that
\mathcal{V}^{\mu}

in Eqs. (
8.38
) and (
8.12
) agree with each
other when
F_{\mu\nu}=0
. Note that the previous constraint
p\cdot a=p^{2}-m^{2}

is satisfied if we take
\widetilde{S}\cdot p=(p\cdot n+m)f_{A}
,
which implies
-\widetilde{S}^{\mu}=p_{\perp}^{\mu}f_{A}/(p\cdot n)

when
m=0
. Thus,
(a\cdot n)/(2p\cdot n)
is identified to be the
helicity in the massless limit.

One can now read out the magnetization-current term in
\mathcal{A}^{\mu}

without background fields from Eq. (
8.38
), i.e., the
second term inside parentheses. We then generalize the derivative
operator to include a background field by analogy with the massless
case
[
151
]
to obtain Eq. (
8.13
), and
find that
\mathcal{A}^{\mu}
has a symmetric form with
\mathcal{V}^{\mu}

under interchanges
p^{\mu}\leftrightarrow a^{\mu}
and
f_{V}\leftrightarrow f_{A}
.
In fact, one could absorb this magnetization-current term into
\widetilde{a}^{\mu}
.
The freedom of such a redefinition reveals itself as the non-uniqueness
of the magnetization-current term as we saw when solving the master
equations (
8.7
) and (
8.8
) for
\mathcal{A}^{\mu}
,
and could occur in the massive case since
\widetilde{a}^{\mu}
is
a dynamical variable to be determined by the kinetic theory. Nevertheless,
it is crucial to explicitly separate the magnetization-current term
from
\widetilde{a}^{\mu}
in order to see a smooth reduction to
the CKT, where
\widetilde{a}^{\mu}
is no longer an independent
dynamical variable and is locked by
p^{\mu}
. See also Refs.
[
388
,
389
]

for related discussions on the connection between massless and massive
fermions.

8.3  Quantum kinetic equations for charge and spin transport

Given the vector and axial-vector components of Wigner functions in
Eqs. (
8.12
) and (
8.13
), we may derive the quantum
kinetic theory (or the so-called axial kinetic theory in Ref.
[
338
]
)
containing a scalar kinetic equation (SKE) and an axial-vector kinetic
equation (AKE) governing the dynamical degrees of freedoms
f_{V/A}

and
a^{\mu}
. Plugging Eq. (
8.12
) into Eq. (
8.4
),
with a complicated yet straightforward calculation (see Ref.
[
338
]

for details), one acquires the SKE with an arbitrary spacetime-dependent
frame vector up to
O(\hbar)
,

\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})p\cdot\Delta f_{V}+\hbar\chi_{0}^{(n)}(a^{\mu},f_{A})+\hbar\chi_{m}^{(n)}(a^{\mu},f_{A})=0,

(8.40)

where the
O(\hbar)
terms
\chi_{0}^{(n)}
and
\chi_{m}^{(n)}

as functions of
a^{\mu}
and
f_{A}
are given by

\displaystyle\chi_{0}^{(n)}(a^{\mu},f_{A})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})\left[\frac{E_{\mu}S_{a(n)}^{\mu\nu}}{p\cdot n}\nabla_{\nu}+S_{a(n)}^{\mu\nu}\left(\partial_{\mu}F_{\rho\nu}\right)\partial_{p}^{\rho}+\left(\partial_{\mu}S_{a(n)}^{\mu\nu}\right)\nabla_{\nu}\right]f_{A}

\displaystyle-\frac{\delta^{\prime}(q^{2}-m^{2})}{p\cdot n}B^{\mu}\Box_{\mu\nu}\widetilde{a}^{\nu},

\displaystyle\chi_{m}^{(n)}(a^{\mu},f_{A})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\delta(q^{2}-m^{2})\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\left\{\nabla_{\mu}\left(\frac{n_{\beta}}{q\cdot n}\right)\left(\nabla_{\nu}a_{\alpha}+F_{\nu\alpha}\right)\right.

(8.41)

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{n_{\beta}}{q\cdot n}\left[(\partial_{\mu}F_{\rho\nu})(\partial_{q}^{\rho}a_{\alpha})+\left(\nabla_{\nu}a_{\alpha}-F_{\rho\nu}(\partial_{p}^{\rho}a_{\alpha})\right)\nabla_{\mu}\right]\right\}f_{A},

with
E^{\mu}
and
B^{\mu}
being defined in Eq. (
4.35
)
with the replacement of
u^{\mu}
by
n^{\mu}
and with
S_{a(n)}^{\mu\nu}

and
\Box_{\mu\nu}
being defined as

\displaystyle S_{a(n)}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}a_{\alpha}n_{\beta}}{2p\cdot n},

\displaystyle\Box_{\mu\nu}\widetilde{a}^{\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p\cdot\nabla\widetilde{a}_{\mu}-F_{\mu\nu}\widetilde{a}^{\nu}.

(8.42)

Similarly, by inserting Eq. (
8.13
) into Eq. (
8.9
),
one derives the AKE also with an arbitrary spacetime-dependent frame
vector up to
O(\hbar)
,

\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})\Box^{\mu\nu}\widetilde{a}_{\nu}+\hbar\chi_{0}^{(n)\mu}(f_{V})+\hbar\chi_{m}^{(n)\mu}(f_{V})=0,

(8.43)

where the
O(\hbar)
terms
\chi_{0}^{(n)\mu}
and
\chi_{m}^{(n)\mu}

as functions
f_{V}
are given by

\displaystyle\chi_{0}^{(n)\mu}(f_{V})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p^{\mu}\left\{\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})\left[\left(\partial_{\alpha}S_{m(n)}^{\alpha\nu}\right)\nabla_{\nu}+\frac{S_{m(n)}^{\alpha\nu}E_{\alpha}\nabla_{\nu}}{p\cdot n+m}+S_{m(n)}^{\rho\nu}(\partial_{\rho}F_{\beta\nu})\partial_{p}^{\beta}\right]\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\delta^{\prime}(p^{2}-m^{2})\frac{p\cdot B}{p\cdot n+m}p\cdot\nabla\right\}f_{V},

\displaystyle\chi_{m}^{(n)\mu}(f_{V})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m\left\{\frac{\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}}{2(p\cdot n+m)}\left[\frac{}{}m(\partial_{\alpha}n_{\beta})\Delta_{\nu}\right.\right.

(8.44)

\displaystyle\left.+(mn_{\beta}+p_{\beta})\left(\frac{E_{\alpha}-\partial_{\alpha}(p\cdot n)}{p\cdot n+m}\nabla_{\nu}-(\partial_{\nu}F_{\rho\alpha})\partial_{p}^{\rho}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\left.+\delta^{\prime}(p^{2}-m^{2})\frac{mn_{\beta}+p_{\beta}}{p\cdot n+m}\tilde{F}^{\mu\beta}p\cdot\nabla\right\}f_{V}.

Taking
\hbar=0
, Eq. (
8.40
) reduces to the Vlasov equation,
while Eq. (
8.43
) corresponds to a relativistic version
of the Bargmann-Michel-Telegdi (BMT) equation
[
395
]
. In
the massless limit, we have
\chi_{m}^{(n)}[q^{\mu},f_{A}]=0
and

\chi_{0}^{(n)}[q^{\mu},f_{A}]
reduces to the
O(\hbar)
terms
in the CKT. On the other hand, for AKE, when
m=0
, we have
\chi_{m}^{(n)\mu}[f_{V}]=0

and the rest part in Eq. (
8.43
) becomes the CKT multiplied
by
p^{\mu}
. As anticipated, Eqs. (
8.40
), and (
8.43
)
make a smooth connection between the massless case delineated by the
CKT and the massive one. As mentioned previously, when choosing the
rest frame of the particle,
n^{\mu}=n_{r}^{\mu}(p)=p^{\mu}/m
, the
magnetization-current term in
\mathcal{A}^{\mu}
vanishes and the
AKE also becomes simpler
[
339
]
, which takes the form,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})\left[\Box_{\mu\nu}\widetilde{a}^{\nu}-\frac{\hbar}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}p_{\rho}(\partial_{\sigma}F_{\beta\nu})\partial_{p}^{\beta}f_{V}\right]+\hbar\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}p_{\nu}\delta^{\prime}(p^{2}-m^{2})p\cdot\nabla f_{V}.

(8.45)

One could also find the equivalent results in Refs.
[
335
,
336
]
.

When solving kinetic equations (
8.40
) and (
8.43
),
we need to handle the terms proportional to derivatives of delta functions

\delta^{\prime}(p^{2}-m^{2})
. One may seek off-shell solutions
of
f_{V}
and
\widetilde{a}^{\nu}
independent of the explicit
expression of
p\cdot n
. In such a case, we can simply take
p\cdot\nabla f_{V}=O(\hbar)

and
\Box^{\mu\nu}\widetilde{a}_{\nu}=O(\hbar)
. Accordingly, the

\delta^{\prime}(p^{2}-m^{2})
terms in Eqs. (
8.40
), and (
8.43
)
are at
O(\hbar^{2})
and thus can be neglected. Nevertheless, when
handling on-shell kinetic equations and the on-shell
f_{V}
and

\widetilde{a}^{\mu}
, the
\delta^{\prime}(p^{2}-m^{2})
terms
cannot simply be omitted. All these terms can be instead arranged
with the leading-order kinetic equations through

\displaystyle 2\delta^{\prime}(p^{2}-m^{2})p^{\mu}p\cdot\nabla f_{V}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})\partial_{p}^{\mu}(p\cdot\nabla f_{V}),

\displaystyle 2\delta^{\prime}(p^{2}-m^{2})\Box_{\mu\nu}\widetilde{a}^{\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})\partial_{p}^{\rho}\left(\frac{n_{\rho}\Box_{\mu\nu}\widetilde{a}^{\nu}}{p\cdot n}\right),

(8.46)

up to
O(\hbar)
. Then, all the delta functions can be factored out
for the SKE and AKE.

8.4  Quantum transport in thermal equilibrium

Given the solutions of quantum kinetic equations, one can obtain the
vector or axial vector current and the symmetric and anti-symmetric
part of the canonical energy-momentum tensor
2
2

2

More precisely, one has to include both fermions and anti-fermions,
and put an overall sign function
{\rm sgn}(p\cdot n)
in front of
the Wigner function.
,

\displaystyle J^{\mu}=4\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mathcal{V}^{\mu},\;\;J_{5}^{\mu}=4\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mathcal{A}^{\mu},

\displaystyle T_{S}^{\mu\nu}=2\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\left(\mathcal{V}^{\mu}p^{\nu}+\mathcal{V}^{\nu}p^{\mu}\right),

\displaystyle T_{A}^{\mu\nu}=2\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\left(\mathcal{V}^{\mu}p^{\nu}-\mathcal{V}^{\nu}p^{\mu}\right).

(8.47)

Angular-momentum conservation arises from Eq. (
8.6
) as discussed
in the massless case
[
175
]
, and
T_{A}^{\mu\nu}
is
responsible for the angular-momentum transfer (see Ref.
[
309
]

and references therein).

While collisionless kinetic equations do not uniquely determine equilibrium
Wigner functions
[
168
]
, we may construct equilibrium
Wigner functions motivated by the following considerations. For the vector
charge, we may naturally take the Fermi distribution function,

f_{V}^{\text{eq}}=f_{\text{FD}}(p\cdot u-\mu)=\frac{1}{\exp[\beta(p\cdot u-\mu)]+1}.

(8.48)

On the other hand, the axial
charge should be damped out as
t\rightarrow\infty
when
m\neq 0

because of the scattering. But
f_{A}^{\text{eq}}
may be at
O(\hbar)

induced by the vorticity correction. Referring to the massless case

[
149
,
168
]
, we also expect that
\mathcal{A}_{\text{eq}}^{\mu}

does not have an explicit dependence on
n^{\mu}
. Thus one may propose
an equilibrium Wigner function in constant magnetic fields and thermal
vorticity
[
396
,
338
,
336
]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{\text{eq}}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})p^{\mu}f_{\text{FD}},

(8.49)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\text{eq}}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi\hbar\left[\frac{1}{4}\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})p_{\nu}\epsilon^{\nu\mu\rho\sigma}\Omega_{\rho\sigma}\partial_{p\cdot\beta}+\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}p_{\nu}\delta^{\prime}(p^{2}-m^{2})\right]f_{\text{FD}}.

(8.50)

Note that the leading-order SKE, merely the Vlasov equation, is satisfied under
the following conditions,

\partial_{(\mu}\beta_{\nu)}=0,\quad\partial_{\mu}\left(\frac{\mu}{T}\right)=F^{\mu\nu}\beta_{\nu},\quad\partial_{\lambda}\Omega_{\mu\nu}=0,

(8.51)

where
A_{(\mu}B_{\nu)}=A_{\mu}B_{\nu}+A_{\nu}B_{\mu}
. In presence
of an electric field
E^{\mu}=F^{\mu\nu}u_{\nu}
in the fluid rest
frame, the system may reach a steady state with an inhomogeneous local
chemical potential. Nonetheless, we here consider an equilibrium state
with
E^{\mu}=0
and hence
\mu/T
is also a constant. The equilibrium

\mathcal{A}_{\text{eq}}^{\mu}
takes the equivalent form as the
one for massless fermions at constant temperature except for the on-shell
condition
[
168
]
, and was also obtained from the statistical
field theory
[
271
]
. See also Refs.
[
397
,
398
]

for Wigner functions beyond weak vorticity and with acceleration.

The equilibrium
\mathcal{A}^{\mu}
in Eq. (
8.50
) now
leads to the CSE current
\hbar J_{(1)}^{\mu}
induced by the vorticity
and magnetic field as in Eq. (
4.32
) but
with the conductivities given by

\displaystyle\xi_{5}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}d|{\bf p}|\frac{2E_{p}^{2}-m^{2}}{E_{p}}\left[f_{\text{FD}}(E_{p}-\mu)+f_{\text{FD}}(E_{p}+\mu)\right],

\displaystyle\xi_{B5}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}d|{\bf p}|\left[f_{\text{FD}}(E_{p}-\mu)-f_{\text{FD}}(E_{p}+\mu)\right].

(8.52)

The above results agree with those derived from the Kubo formula with
thermal correlators
[
399
,
159
]
.

9  Quantum kinetic theory for massive fermions with collisions

In this section, we include collision terms into the QKT to describe
spin transport phenomena. Similar to the massless case
[
151
]
,
one may obtain a generic form of collision terms in self-energies
based on the Kadanoff-Baym equation
[
339
,
342
]
.
Alternatively, one may apply a distinct formalism with generalized
distribution functions in extended phase space including spin degrees
of freedom and with non-local collisions
[
258
,
337
,
341
]
.
A practical power counting scheme is adopted to treat spin degrees
of freedom in a subdominant order in the vector charge or energy transport
equations. There are several other studies of collisional effects
pertinent to spin polarization in heavy-ion collisions from different
or relevant approaches
[
184
,
400
,
401
,
158
,
189
,
319
,
402
,
403
,
404
,
405
]
.
Here we will mostly focus on the derivation from the Kadanoff-Baym
equation and that from non-local collisions.

9.1  Effective quantum kinetic equations

To include collisions into the QKT for massive fermions, we have to
incorporate non-vanishing self-energies in the Kadanoff-Baym equation
shown in Eq. (
8.2
). In analogy with the massless
case, we only focus on scattering processes, so we drop the real parts
of retarded and advanced self-energies and of retarded propagators, see Eqs. (
7.32
), (
7.33
), and (
7.37
) and accompanied discussions.
One then follows the same recipe as in the collisionless case to carry
out the decomposition of Wigner functions and derive master equations
with
\hbar
expansion. For collision terms, we further decompose
the self-energies in the same way as for Wigner functions,

\Sigma^{\lessgtr}=\Sigma_{F}^{\lessgtr}+i\Sigma_{P}^{\lessgtr}\gamma^{5}+\Sigma_{V\mu}^{\lessgtr}\gamma^{\mu}+\Sigma_{A\mu}^{\lessgtr}\gamma^{5}\gamma^{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}\Sigma_{T\mu\nu}^{\lessgtr}\sigma^{\mu\nu}.

(9.1)

Inserting Eqs. (
8.1
), and (
9.1
) into
Eq. (
8.2
) with proper arrangements like the collisionless
case, we again acquire 10 coupled equations. Making similar replacements
as Eq. (
8.3
) to rewrite
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{P}
,
and
\mathcal{S}^{\mu\nu}
in terms of
\mathcal{V}^{\mu}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\mu}
,
we further obtain 6 independent master equations. Unlike the collisionless
case, these equations are more involved to find the perturbative solution
and derive the quantum kinetic equations. Nonetheless, for practical
purposes, we may propose a useful power-counting scheme to simplify
the problem.

In most of the practical situations such as in heavy-ion collisions, the
axial-charge current is usually smaller than the vector-charge current
since the spin polarization is generated by quantum effects. This
observation motivates us to introduce the power counting assumption

[
339
]
,

\mathcal{V}_{\mu}^{\lessgtr}\sim O(\hbar^{0})\quad{\rm and}\quad\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\lessgtr}\sim O(\hbar).

(9.2)

Hereafter we attach the superscript
\lessgtr
to specify the lesser
and greater components. The above power counting assumption also constrains
the orders of other components from master equations,

\mathcal{F}^{\lessgtr}\sim O(\hbar^{0}),\quad\mathcal{S}_{\mu\nu}^{\lessgtr}\sim O(\hbar),\quad\mathcal{P}^{\lessgtr}\sim O(\hbar^{2}).

(9.3)

On the other hand, since
\Sigma_{F}^{\lessgtr}
and
\Sigma_{V,\mu}^{\lessgtr}

are responsible for the collision terms of classical Boltzmann equations,
we should take

\Sigma_{F}^{\lessgtr}\sim O(\hbar^{0})\quad{\rm and}\quad\Sigma_{V\mu}^{\lessgtr}\sim O(\hbar^{0}),

(9.4)

which further results in

\Sigma_{A\mu}^{\lessgtr}\sim O(\hbar^{1})\quad{\rm and}\quad\Sigma_{T\mu\nu}^{\lessgtr}\sim O(\hbar^{1}),

(9.5)

by balancing the orders of coupled equations. However, there exists
no explicit restriction for
\Sigma_{P}^{\lessgtr}
in generic master
equations, while it is expected that the presence of
\Sigma_{P}^{\lessgtr}

has to be induced by nonzero pseudo-scalar condensate, which should
be at
O(\hbar^{2})
from the consistency with the anomaly equation
(mass correction upon the chiral anomaly), see Ref.
[
339
]

for technical details. When adopting this power counting scheme, we
shall distinguish the explicit quantum correction dubbed as the explicit
\hbar

term with
\hbar
attached to it (
\hbar\mathcal{C}_{2}^{(n)\mu}
) and the implicit one without
\hbar
attached (
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{(n)\mu}
) although they are now of the same order in
\hbar
expansion. The latter further incorporates the "classical contribution" (
\mathcal{C}^{\mu}_{\text{cl}}
), which refers to the scattering with a classical medium without
\hbar
corrections. Such a term is still of
O(\hbar)
due to the involvement of
\tilde{a}^{\mu}
from the probe fermion. Detailed expressions of
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{(n)\mu}
,
\mathcal{C}_{2}^{(n)\mu}
, and
\mathcal{C}^{\mu}_{\text{cl}}
will be shown later.

By implementing the power counting, the master equations obtained
from the Kadanoff-Baym equations at the leading order are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\mu}\mathcal{V}_{\mu}^{<}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{m}p_{\mu}\widehat{\Sigma_{F}\mathcal{V}^{\mu}},

(9.6)

\displaystyle p^{[\mu}\mathcal{V}^{<\nu]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(9.7)

\displaystyle(p^{2}-m^{2})\mathcal{V}_{\mu}^{<}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(9.8)

\displaystyle p\cdot\mathcal{A}^{<}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(9.9)

\displaystyle(p^{2}-m^{2})\mathcal{A}^{<\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\hbar}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\alpha\beta\gamma}p_{\alpha}D_{\beta}\mathcal{V}_{\gamma}^{<},

(9.10)

\displaystyle p\cdot\mathcal{D}\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{<}-F_{\mu\nu}\mathcal{A}^{<\nu}-\frac{\hbar}{4}\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}[\mathcal{D}^{\nu},\mathcal{D}^{\rho}]\mathcal{V}^{<\sigma}

\displaystyle=-m\Big(\widehat{\Sigma_{F}\mathcal{A}_{\mu}}-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\widehat{\Sigma_{T}^{\nu\rho}\mathcal{V}^{\sigma}}\Big)-p_{\alpha}\widehat{\Sigma_{\mu}^{A}\mathcal{V}^{\alpha}}+p_{\mu}\widehat{\Sigma_{\alpha}^{A}\mathcal{V}^{\alpha}},

(9.11)

where we have defined
\mathcal{D}_{\mu}Z=\nabla_{\mu}Z+\widehat{\Sigma_{V\mu}Z}

and
\widehat{XY}=X^{>}Y^{<}-X^{<}Y^{>}
with
X
,
Y
, and
Z

being the coefficients of the Clifford decomposition for propagators
and self-energies. Note that we have to keep the
O(\hbar)
terms
linear to
\mathcal{V}_{\mu}^{\lessgtr}
in Eqs. (
9.10
) and
(
9.11
) since
\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\lessgtr}\sim O(\hbar)
.
In addition, we consider weakly coupled systems and thus drop the

O(\Sigma^{2})
terms. Since now we only focus on leading-order results,
we neglected quantum corrections of
\mathcal{V}_{\mu}^{<}
. Apparently,

\mathcal{V}_{\mu}^{<}
is led by the classical solution in Eq. (
8.10
),
whereas the SKE from Eq. (
9.6
) now reads

\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})\left(p\cdot\Delta f_{V}+p_{\mu}\widehat{\Sigma_{V}^{\mu}f_{V}}+m\widehat{\Sigma_{F}f_{V}}\right)=0,

(9.12)

as a standard Boltzmann equation, where
f_{V}^{<}=f_{V}
and
f_{V}^{>}=1-f_{V}
.
On the other hand, similar to the collisionless case, the magnetization-current
term in
\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\lessgtr}
cannot be determined by Eqs. (
9.9
)
and (
9.10
). We may, however propose a generalized solution in
connection with the massless solution. It is postulated
[
339
]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{<\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi\left[\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})\left(a^{\mu}f_{A}+\hbar S_{m(n)}^{\mu\nu}\mathcal{D}_{\nu}f_{V}\right)\right.

(9.13)

\displaystyle\left.+\hbar\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}p_{\nu}\delta^{\prime}(p^{2}-m^{2})f_{V}\right],

which is a generalization of
\mathcal{A}^{<\mu}
in Eq. (
8.13
)
with the replacement of
\nabla_{\nu}f_{V}
by
\mathcal{D}_{\nu}f_{V}
.
The generalization of the magnetization-current term with collisions
here is rather natural since
\Sigma_{V\mu}^{\lessgtr}
are the only
vectors at
O(\hbar^{0})
in self-energies, which can then be coupled
to
S_{m(n)}^{\mu\nu}
. Furthermore, for the solution of
\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{>}
,
we interchange the lesser and greater components therein, e.g., we
replace
f_{A}=f_{A}^{<}
and
f_{V}=f_{V}^{<}
in Eq. (
9.13
)
by
f_{V}^{>}=1-f_{V}
and
f_{A}^{>}=-f_{A}
due to its origin
from the expectation value of the fermionic density operator in spinor
space.

Inserting Eq. (
9.13
) into Eq. (
9.11
),
the AKE with collisional effects and the general frame vector
n^{\mu}=n^{\mu}(x)

takes the form,

\displaystyle\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})\Box^{\mu\nu}\widetilde{a}_{\nu}+\hbar\chi_{0}^{(n)\mu}[f_{V}]+\hbar\chi_{m}^{(n)\mu}[f_{V}]=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{(n)\mu}+\hbar\mathcal{C}_{2}^{(n)\mu}.

(9.14)

The collisionless part is given in Eq. (
8.45
). The collision
terms are

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{1}^{(n)\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})\left[a^{\mu}p_{\nu}\widehat{\Sigma_{V}^{\nu}f_{A}}+m^{2}\widehat{\Sigma_{A}^{\mu}f_{V}}-p^{\mu}p_{\nu}\widehat{\Sigma_{A}^{\nu}f_{V}}+\right.

(9.15)

\displaystyle+m\left(a^{\mu}\widehat{\Sigma_{F}f_{A}}-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}p_{\nu}\widehat{\Sigma_{T\rho\sigma}f_{V}}\right),

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{2}^{(n)\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})\left\{\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}p_{\nu}(\nabla_{\rho}\widehat{\Sigma_{V\sigma})f_{V}}\right.

(9.16)

\displaystyle+2S_{m(n)}^{\mu\nu}\left[m(\nabla_{\nu}\widehat{\Sigma_{F})f_{V}}+F_{\,\,\,\nu}^{\rho}\widehat{\Sigma_{V\rho}f_{V}}-(p\cdot\Delta\widehat{\Sigma_{V\nu})f_{V}}\right]

\displaystyle\left.+2\widehat{f_{V}\Sigma_{V\nu}}\left(p\cdot\nabla S_{m(n)}^{\mu\nu}-F_{\,\,\,\lambda}^{\mu}S_{m(n)}^{\lambda\nu}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle-\left[\left(2S_{m(n)}^{\mu\nu}p^{\lambda}F_{\lambda\nu}-\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}p_{\nu}\right)\delta^{\prime}(p^{2}-m^{2})+\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})\left(\nabla_{\nu}S_{m(n)}^{\mu\nu}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\times\left(C_{V}[f_{V}]+mC_{S}[f_{V}]\right),

where
C_{V}[f_{V}]=-p^{\mu}\widehat{\Sigma_{V\mu}f_{V}}
and
C_{S}[f_{V}]=-\widehat{\Sigma_{F}f_{V}}
.
Note that
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{(n)\mu}
implicitly contains the
\hbar

terms, from e.g.,
\Sigma_{A}^{\mu}
and
\Sigma_{T\rho\sigma}
,
and hence depends on the frame choice. In general, purely classical
contribution from
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{(n)\mu}
only results in spin
relaxation and diffusion
3
3

3

In Ref.
[
339
]
, both effects are less accurately dubbed as diffusion except for the case of heavy quarks, where only the diffusion term remains.
, whereas the implicit
\hbar
terms therein and the explicit
ones from
\mathcal{C}_{2}^{(n)\mu}
as quantum corrections could
yield dynamical spin polarization originating from, e.g., the spatial
inhomogeneity of
f_{V}
like vorticity.

9.2  Simplifications and analyses of collision terms

Although
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{(n)\mu}+\hbar\mathcal{C}_{2}^{(n)\mu}

given by Eqs. (
9.15
) and (
9.16
) serves as generic
collision terms in the presence of spacetime-dependent background electromagnetic
fields and an arbitrary spacetime-dependent frame vector
n^{\mu}(x)
,
we may consider simplified expressions for practical purposes
[
339
]
.
Based on the frame independence of Wigner functions and physical results,
it is more convenient to work with a constant frame vector such that

\partial_{\mu}n^{\nu}=0
. More precisely, we could simply take
n^{\mu}=(1,{\bf 0})
,
which also corresponds to the frame choice for the CKT presented in
early works obtained from the Berry phase
[
146
,
144
,
145
]
.
For an application to the spin transport of quarks traveling in the
QGP, one may further neglect the influences from electromagnetic fields
that decay in late stages
[
21
,
406
]
. The
simplified AKE can be put into the form,

\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})p\cdot\partial\widetilde{a}^{\mu}=\mathcal{C}_{s1}^{(n)\mu}+\hbar\mathcal{C}_{s2}^{(n)\mu},

(9.17)

where the collision terms
\mathcal{C}_{s1}^{(n)\mu}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{(n)\mu}

despite the implicit quantum corrections therein and
\mathcal{C}_{s2}^{(n)\mu}

is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{s2}^{(n)\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})\left\{\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}p_{\nu}(\partial_{\rho}\widehat{\Sigma_{V\sigma})f_{V}}\right.

(9.18)

\displaystyle+2S_{m(n)}^{\mu\nu}\left[m(\partial_{\nu}\widehat{\Sigma_{F})f_{V}}+p^{\rho}(\partial_{\nu}\widehat{\Sigma_{V\rho})f_{V}}-(p\cdot\partial\widehat{\Sigma_{V\nu})f_{V}}\right].

By rearranging the explicit
\hbar
corrections in Eq. (
9.17
)
based on the decomposition for the terms proportional to
p^{\mu}

and
m
, respectively,
\mathcal{C}_{s2}^{(n)\mu}
can be equivalently
written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{s2}^{(n)\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})\left\{p^{\mu}S_{m(n)}^{\rho\nu}\widehat{(\partial_{\rho}\Sigma_{V\nu})f_{V}}\right.

(9.19)

\displaystyle\left.-m\left[S_{m(n)}^{\mu\nu}(\partial_{\nu}\widehat{\Sigma_{F})f_{V}}+\frac{\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}(p_{\rho}+mn_{\rho})}{2(p\cdot n+m)}(\partial_{\sigma}\widehat{\Sigma_{V\nu})f_{V}}\right]\right\}.

Although the collision term (
9.18
) and (
9.19
) are
mathematically equivalent, the collision term (
9.18
)
is more useful in the large-mass regime while the term (
9.19
)
is more suitable for light fermions.

In addition, when focusing on massive fermions with a mass much greater
than the gradient scale, we can set the frame vector at their rest
frame
n^{\mu}=n_{r}^{\mu}(p)=p^{\mu}/m
to simplify both the Wigner
functions and AKE. Note that such a frame choice is also used in Refs.
[
336
,
335
]
.
In such a case, the magnetization-current term in
\mathcal{A}^{<\mu}

vanishes and thus
\mathcal{A}^{<\mu}
reduces to

\mathcal{A}^{<\mu}=2\pi\left[\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})a^{\mu}f_{A}+\hbar\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}p_{\nu}\delta^{\prime}(p^{2}-m^{2})f_{V}\right],

(9.20)

which is same as the form without collisions. From Eq. (
9.11
),
we obtain the AKE in the form of Eq. (
9.14
) with

n^{\mu}
replaced by
n_{r}^{\mu}(p)=p^{\mu}/m
but with
\mathcal{C}_{2}^{(n_{r})\mu}

defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{2}^{(n_{r})\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}p_{\nu}(\Delta_{\rho}\widehat{\Sigma_{V,\sigma})f_{V}}

(9.21)

\displaystyle-\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}p_{\nu}\delta^{\prime}(p^{2}-m^{2})(p\cdot\widehat{\Sigma_{V}f_{V}}+m\widehat{\Sigma_{F}f_{V}}).

Recall that the choice of a rest frame is valid only when the mass
is much larger than the gradient and electromagnetic scales in the
system. Here the quantum correction on collisions in Eq. (
9.21
)
is also present in Eq. (
9.16
). More succinctly, Eqs. (
9.21
)
and (
9.18
) share the same term
(1/2)\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}p_{\nu}(\partial_{\rho}\widehat{\Sigma_{V\sigma})f_{V}}

when neglecting electric/magnetic fields though Eq. (
9.18
)
contains extra terms. We may regard these extra terms in Eq. (
9.16
)
or Eq. (
9.18
) as the
O(|{\bf p}|/m)
corrections on top
of Eq. (
9.21
).

In summary, the leading-order SKE remains the same as a classical
Boltzmann equation, whereas the AKE governing the dynamics of spin
polarization. The AKE (
9.14
) delineates the evolution
of an axial-vector component
\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{<}
in Wigner functions
with collisional effects characterized by
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{(n)\mu}+\hbar\mathcal{C}_{2}^{(n)\mu}=\mathcal{C}_{\text{cl}}^{\mu}+\hbar\mathcal{C}_{\text{Q}}^{(n)\mu}
.
It is found
\mathcal{C}_{\text{cl}}^{\mu}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\text{Q}}^{(n)\mu}

are proportional to
f_{A}
and
f_{V}
, respectively. Consequently,

\mathcal{C}_{\text{cl}}^{\mu}
serves as the term of spin relaxation and diffusion,
which vanishes when
f_{A}=0
. Such a fact will be explicitly shown
in the case for massive quarks traversing weakly coupled QGP in the
next subsection. On the contrary, the “quantum” correction
\mathcal{C}_{\text{Q}}^{(n)\mu}
,
which is led by the explicit
\hbar
term such as
\mathcal{C}_{2}^{(n)\mu}

and the implicit
\hbar
corrections in
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{(n)\mu}
,
survives when
f_{A}=0
. It is dubbed as the spin-polarization term
and responsible for the polarization of particles via the intertwined
dynamics of vector-charge transport due to spin-orbit interaction.
Albeit not in QCD, a concrete example can be found in the application
of AKE to the Nambu-Jona-Lasinio model
[
407
,
343
]
, where
the equilibrium value of
\mathcal{A}^{\mu}
as in Eq. (
8.50
)
triggered by nonzero vorticity is acquired via the detailed balance
with a vanishing collision term. See also Refs.
[
181
,
347
]
for follow-up studies in the gauge theory for massless and massive fermions. The so-called non-local collisions
also lead to equivalent results
[
337
,
341
]
, which will be further elaborated in the next section.

9.3  Spin relaxation and diffusion in weakly coupled QGP

We now apply the formalism established in previous sections to investigate
the collision terms for massive quarks traversing weakly coupled QGP
in relativistic heavy-ion collisions. Nevertheless, to obtain explicit
expressions of
\hbar
corrections in the collision terms for QGP
is rather challenging. Even though the implicit
\hbar
corrections
encoded in self-energies from light quarks in equilibrium could be
derived from equilibrium Wigner functions shown in, e.g., Refs.
[
168
,
338
]
,
how to include analogous corrections led by vorticity from polarized
gluons is currently unknown. For the future application on the dynamical
spin polarization of strange quarks traversing QGP, we will have to
work out the Wigner functions for polarized gluons up to
O(\hbar)

with both classical and quantum contributions at least in equilibrium
as one of the essential ingredients. Recently, there have been related
studies for the Wigner functions and QKT of polarized photons in Refs.
[
408
,
409
]
,
but the direct application to QCD is still not feasible. In this subsection,
we will only compute the classical contribution in the collision terms
responsible for spin relaxation and diffusion in weakly coupled QGP, which was first
derived in Ref.
[
400
]
and reproduced in Ref.
[
339
]

with a more complete form.

To evaluate the classical contribution
\textcolor
blue
\mathcal{C}^{\mu}_{\text{cl}}
in
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{(n)\mu}
,
we have to first derive the fermionic self-energies therein. In the
following, we call fermions quarks and gauge bosons gluons interchangeably,
and include the color-group factors. Here, the color degrees of freedom
do not play a crucial role (like in the color conductivity), and the
same computation holds for QED with simple replacements of relevant
degrees of freedom. Furthermore, we consider the massive quarks with
their mass much greater than the scale of thermal mass in QGP and
accordingly neglect the Compton scattering with gluons as subleading
effects analogous to the study of heavy-quark transport in heavy-ion
collisions (see, e.g., Ref.
[
410
]
).

We shall consider a massive fermion, which may be regarded as a strange
quark, probing the weakly coupled QGP dominated by massless quarks
and gluons in thermal equilibrium. Having assumed the weakly coupled
system, we focus on the lowest-order contributions in the coupling
constant
g_{c}
, i.e., the 2-to-2 scatterings between a massive
quark and a massless quark/gluon. Since the computation is rather
complicated and lengthy, we will briefly mention the theoretical setup
with some critical steps of the derivation and highlight the primary
results. One may refer to Refs.
[
400
,
339
]
for
technical details.

The fermionic self-energies from gluon-exchange processes between
a massive fermion and the medium can be written as

\Sigma^{\lessgtr}(x,p)=\lambda_{c}\int_{p^{\prime}}\gamma^{\mu}W^{\lessgtr}(x,p^{\prime})\gamma^{\nu}G_{\mu\nu}^{\gtrless}(x,p-p^{\prime}),

(9.22)

where
\lambda_{c}
denotes an overall coefficient including the
coupling and we dropped
O(\hbar^{2})
and higher-order corrections
in our power counting. The gluon propagator
G_{\mu\nu}^{\lessgtr}

in the Coulomb gauge and the fluid rest frame is given by

G_{\mu\nu}^{\lessgtr}(x,q)=g_{\text{eq},q}^{\lessgtr}\Big[\rho_{L}(q)P_{\mu\nu}^{L}+\rho_{T}(q)P_{\mu\nu}^{T}\Big],

(9.23)

where
q^{\mu}=p^{\mu}-p^{\prime\mu}
denotes the four-momentum transfer
in scatterings,
g_{\text{eq},q}^{<}=g_{0q}=1/(e^{\beta q\cdot u}-1)
,
and
g_{\text{eq},q}^{>}=1+g_{0q}
. Here we introduce the longitudinal
and transverse projectors,

P_{\mu\nu}^{L}\equiv u_{\mu}u_{\nu},\quad P_{\mu\nu}^{T}\equiv-\Theta_{\mu\alpha}\Theta_{\nu\beta}\left(\eta^{\alpha\beta}+\frac{q^{\alpha}q^{\beta}}{|{\bf q}|^{2}}\right)=-\left(\Theta_{\mu\nu}+\frac{q_{\perp\mu}q_{\perp\nu}}{|{\bf q}|^{2}}\right),

(9.24)

where
\Theta^{\mu\nu}\equiv\eta^{\mu\nu}-u^{\mu}u^{\nu}
with
u^{\mu}\approx(1,\bm{0})
.
We also adopt the hard-thermal-loop (HTL) approximation such that

g_{c}T\ll|q^{\mu}|\ll T
, from which the HTL gluon spectral densities
take the form as (see, e.g., Ref.
[
411
]
)

\rho_{L}(q)\approx\frac{\pi m_{D}^{2}q_{0}}{|\bm{q}|^{5}},\quad\rho_{T}(q)\approx\frac{\pi m_{D}^{2}q_{0}}{2|\bm{q}|^{5}\left(1-q_{0}^{2}/|\mathbf{q}|^{2}\right)},

(9.25)

where
m_{D}\sim g_{c}T
corresponds to the Debye mass. The explicit
form of
m_{D}
and
\lambda_{c}
for gluons in
SU(N_{c})
color
group and
N_{f}
-flavored massless quarks are given by

m_{D}^{2}=\frac{g_{c}^{2}T^{2}(2N_{c}+N_{f})}{6},\quad\lambda_{c}=g_{c}^{2}C_{2}(F)=\frac{g_{c}^{2}(N_{c}^{2}-1)}{2N_{c}}.

(9.26)

Given the explicit form of the gluon propagators
G_{\mu\nu}^{\lessgtr}(x,q)

and fermionic Wigner functions
W^{\lessgtr}(x,p^{\prime})
, for
which only the classical contributions are included, one can derive
the fermionic self-energies
\Sigma^{\lessgtr}(x,p)
. Making the
decomposition of
\Sigma^{\lessgtr}(x,p)
in light of Eq. (
9.1
)
and inserting all ingredients into Eq. (
9.12
) and
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{(n)\mu}

in Eq. (
9.15
) in the absence of background fields, one then
derives the classical collision terms for the SKE and AKE. Based on
the HTL approximation, in which one should conduct the small-
q

expansion when evaluating the momentum integral in the collision kernel
with the infrared cutoff around
g_{c}T
, the leading-logarithmic
results in
g_{c}
of collision terms are acquired
[
400
,
339
]
.
The SKE takes the form,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta(p^{2}-m^{2})\left[p\cdot\partial-\kappa_{{\rm LL}}\left(2-2f_{V}+c_{(1)}\widetilde{p}_{\perp}^{\beta}\partial_{p_{\perp}^{\beta}}\right.\right.

(9.27)

\displaystyle\left.\left.+c_{(2)}\widetilde{p}_{\perp}^{\alpha}\widetilde{p}_{\perp}^{\beta}\partial_{p_{\perp}^{\alpha}}\partial_{p_{\perp}^{\beta}}+c_{(3)}\eta^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{p_{\perp}^{\alpha}}\partial_{p_{\perp}^{\beta}}\right)\right]f_{V},

where
